{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
.

6.3.1.
The rectangles in
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
.

We need to establish that after cutting our rectangles along the stable intervals determined by the family
\mathcal{W}
,
\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{W})
, the resulting connected components are all rectangles. The next lemma is key to this property.

Lemma 20
.

Let
\tilde{R}
be the closure of a connected component of
\overset{o}{R_{i}}\setminus{\mathcal{I}}({\mathcal{W}})
. Then
\tilde{R}
is a horizontal sub-rectangle of
R_{i}

Proof.

If
i\neq j
, then
\overset{o}{R_{i}}\cap\cup{\mathcal{I}}(j,\underline{w})=\emptyset
for all
\underline{w}\in{\mathcal{W}}
, because for any point
x\in R_{i}
and every code
\underline{v}\in\pi^{-1}_{(f,{\mathcal{R}})}(x)
,
v_{0}=i
. In contrast, if
y\in\cup{\mathcal{I}}(j,\underline{w})
, at least one code
\underline{w}^{\prime}=\sigma^{t}(\underline{w})
in
\pi^{-1}(x)
has its zero term equal to
j
, i.e.,
w^{\prime}_{0}=j
. Therefore,
x\neq y
.

We only need to consider the case when
I_{t,\underline{w}}\subset R_{i}
, for
\underline{w}\in{\mathcal{W}}
. In this situation,
I_{t,\underline{w}}
is a horizontal interval of
R_{i}
. Consequently, each connected component of
\overset{o}{R_{i}}\setminus\cup{\mathcal{I}}(i,\underline{w})
is a horizontal sub-rectangle
H
of
R_{i}
minus its stable boundaries, and its closure coincides with
H
. This argument proves our lemma.
∎

Definition 50
.

The family, which includes all the rectangles as described in Lemma
20
, corresponding to all the rectangles
R_{i}
in
{\mathcal{R}}
, is denoted is denoted by
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
.

Lemma 21
.

The family of rectangles
\mathcal{R}_{S(\mathcal{W})}
is a Markov partition for
f
, consisting of horizontal sub-rectangles of the rectangles in
\mathcal{R}
.

Proof.

The interiors of two distinct horizontal sub-rectangles within
R_{i}
do not intersect, and a horizontal sub-rectangle from
R_{i}
does not overlap with the interior of a horizontal sub-rectangle from
R_{j}
if
j\neq i
. Consequently, the interiors of two different rectangles in
{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{W}}}
are disjoint.

The union
\cup{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
coincides with the union of the rectangles in
{\mathcal{R}}
, but
{\mathcal{R}}
is Markov partition for
f
, therefore
\cup{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}=\cup{\mathcal{R}}=S
.

The stable boundary of
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
consists of two types of stable intervals: those that are part of the stable boundary of
{\mathcal{R}}
and whose image is contained in
\partial^{s}{\mathcal{R}}\subset\partial^{s}{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
, and those of the form
I_{t,\underline{w}^{q}}
for some
\underline{w}^{q}\in{\mathcal{W}}
that, as stated in Lemma
19
:
f(I_{t,\underline{w}^{q}})\subset I_{t+1,\underline{w}^{q}}\subset\partial^{s}{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
. Therefore,
\partial^{s}{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
is
f
-invariant.

Since the unstable boundary of
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
coincides with the unstable boundary of
{\mathcal{R}}
,
\partial^{u}{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
is
f^{-1}
-invariant.

This proves that
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
is a Markov partition of
f
.
∎

Remark 2
.

If the periodic code
\underline{w}\in\mathcal{W}
is an
s
-boundary code, Item
ii)
of Lemma
18
states that every interval in the set
\mathcal{I}(i,\underline{w})
is contained within the stable boundary of
R_{i}
. Consequently, the set of rectangles obtained by cutting the rectangle
R_{i}\in\mathcal{R}
through the segments in
\mathcal{I}(i,\mathcal{W})
coincides with
R_{i}
. Therefore, from now on, we are going to assume that all the codes in
\mathcal{W}
are not
s
-boundary codes.

6.3.2.
The relative position of the rectangles: One code.

We must assume for a while that
\mathcal{W}:=\{\underline{w}\}
contains only one code in order to prove some technical lemmas. The generalizations are straightforward but will be introduced later, as this simpler case is sufficient to gain intuition.

In order to have a geometric refinement, we must assign a labeling to the rectangles of
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{W})}
that is coherent with the vertical orientation of the rectangles in
\mathcal{R}
. We can do this by declaring that for a pair of rectangles
R_{1},R_{2}\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{W})}
contained in
R_{i}
, we have the labels
R_{(i,s_{1})}=R_{1}
and
R_{(i,s_{2})}=R_{2}
with
s_{1}<s_{2}
if and only if, with respect to the vertical direction of
R_{i}
, we have
R_{1}<_{v(i)}R_{2}
. However, this procedure is not algorithmic; therefore, we need to develop a method to determine when two different stable intervals in
\mathcal{I}(i,\mathcal{W})
are one above the other. For this reason, the first thing we are going to determine is when two horizontal intervals induced by
\underline{w}\in\mathcal{W}
and contained in the same rectangle
R_{i}\in\mathcal{R}
are one above the other with respect to the vertical orientation of
R_{i}
.

Definition 51
.

Let
\underline{w}\in\mathcal{W}
be a non-
s
-boundary periodic code with period
P
. Then, for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,P-1\}
, we define:

(23)

\mathcal{O}(i,\underline{w})=\{(t,\underline{w}):t\in\{0,\ldots,P-1\}\text{ and }w_{t}=i\}.

The cardinality of this set is denoted by
O(i,\underline{w})
. Similarly, let

(24)

\mathcal{O}(i,\mathcal{W})=\bigcup_{\underline{w}\in\mathcal{W}}\mathcal{O}(i,\underline{w}).

Let
O(i,\mathcal{W})
be its cardinality.

Let us recall that
\mathcal{H}(T)
and
\mathcal{V}(T)
are the sets of horizontal and vertical labels for the geometric type
T
, and that we are always assuming
A(T)
is binary.

Lemma 22
.

Let
\underline{w}\in\mathcal{W}
with period
P
and take
t\in\{1,\cdots,P\}
. There is a unique index
j_{t,\underline{w}}\in\{1,\cdots,h_{w_{t}}\}
that satisfies the following condition:
(w_{t},j_{t,\underline{w}})\in\mathcal{H}(T)
is the unique pair of indices for which
\rho_{T}(w_{t},j_{t,\underline{w}})=(w_{t+1},l_{t+1})\in\mathcal{V}(T)
(for some
l_{t+1}\in\{1,\cdots,v_{w_{t+1}}\}
). Furthermore,
l_{t+1}\in\{1,\cdots,v_{w_{t+1}}\}
is also uniquely determined.

Proof.

Let’s suppose that
w_{t}=i
. As the incidence matrix of
T
is binary, there exists at most one horizontal sub-rectangle
H^{i}_{j}
of
R_{i}
such that
f(H^{i}_{j})\subset R_{w_{t+1}}
. However, since
\underline{w}
is an admissible code, there exists at least one horizontal sub-rectangle
H^{i}_{j}
of
R_{i}
such that
f(H^{i}_{j})\subset R_{w_{t+1}}
. Therefore, there exists only one rectangle of
R_{i}
such that
f(H^{i}_{j})\subset R_{w_{t+1}}
, and we declare
j_{t,\underline{w}}
as this unique number. Clearly, if
\rho_{T}(i,j_{t,\underline{w}})=(w_{t+1},l_{t+1})
, then
l_{t+1}\in\{1,\cdots,h_{w_{t+1}}\}
is uniquely determined.
∎

Corollary 11
.

Assume that
w_{t}=i
, then the stable interval
I_{t,\underline{w}}
is contained in the horizontal sub-rectangle
H^{i}_{j_{t,\underline{w}}}\setminus\partial^{s}H^{i}_{j_{t,\underline{w}}}
of
{\mathcal{R}}
.

Proof.

It is evident that
\pi_{f}(\underline{w})\in H^{i}_{j_{t,\underline{w}}}
. Now, consider another code
\underline{v}\in\underline{F}^{s}(\sigma^{t}(\underline{w}))
that projects to
I_{t,\underline{w}}
. As in Lemma
22
, there exists only one index
j_{t,\underline{v}}\in\{1,\ldots,h_{v_{t}}\}
such that
\rho_{T}(i,j_{t,\underline{v}})=(v_{t+1},l^{\prime})
, and therefore
\pi_{f}(\underline{v})\in H^{i}_{j_{t,\underline{v}}}
. However, due to the matching of non-negative codes between
\underline{w}
and
\underline{v}
, we have
w_{t}=v_{t}
and
w_{t+1}=v_{t+1}
, leading to
j_{t,\underline{v}}=j_{t,\underline{w}}
. Consequently,
\pi_{f}(\underline{v})\in H^{i}_{j_{t,\underline{w}}}
.

Since
\underline{w}
is not an
s
-boundary point, the projection of its stable leaf codes cannot intersect the stable leaves of
s
-boundary periodic points. In particular, it does not intersect the stable boundary of the horizontal sub-rectangles of
(f,{\mathcal{R}})
. Hence, we deduce that
I_{t,\underline{w}}\subset H^{i}_{j_{t,\underline{w}}}\setminus\partial^{s}H^{i}_{j_{t,\underline{w}}}
. This confirms the claim.

∎

Lemma 23
.

Let
I_{t_{1},\underline{w}}
and
I_{t_{2},\underline{w}}
be two stable segments contained within
R_{i}
, i.e.,
w_{t_{1}}=w_{t_{2}}=i
. Suppose that
j_{t_{1},\underline{w}}<_{v(i)}j_{t_{2},\underline{w}}
. Then, with respect to the vertical orientation of
R_{i}
:

I_{t_{1},\underline{w}}<_{v(i)}I_{t_{2},\underline{w}}.

Proof.

As was observed in Corollary
11
,
I_{t_{1},\underline{w}}\subset H^{i}_{j_{t_{1},\underline{w}}}
and
I_{t_{2},\underline{w}}\subset H^{i}_{j_{t_{2},\underline{w}}}
. With respect to the vertical orientation of
R_{i}
any stable interval contained in
H^{i}_{j_{t_{1},\underline{w}}}
lies below any stable interval contained in
H^{i}_{j_{t_{2},\underline{w}}}
. In particular, we have
I_{t_{1},\underline{w}}<I_{t_{2},\underline{w}}
, as we claimed.
∎

Lemma 24
.

Let
\underline{w}\in\mathcal{W}
with period
P
. If there exist two distinct numbers
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
in
\{0,\cdots,P-1\}
such that
\sigma^{t_{1}}(\underline{w})\neq\sigma^{t_{2}}(\underline{w})
but both have
w_{t_{1}}=w_{t_{2}}=i
, then we consider the two elements in
\mathcal{O}(i,\underline{w})
determined by these iterations:
(t_{1},\underline{w})
and
(t_{2},\underline{w})
. In this case:

i)

There exists an natural number
M
between
1
and
P-1
such that
w_{t_{1}+M}\neq w_{t_{2}+M}
, but for all
m
from
0
to
M-1
,
w_{t_{1}+m}=w_{t_{2}+m}
.

ii)

If
M=1
, then
j_{t_{1},\underline{w}}\neq j_{t_{2},\underline{w}}
. However, if
M\geq 2
, for all
m\in\{0,\cdots,M-2\}
, the indices
j_{t_{1}+m,\underline{w}}
and
j_{t_{2}+m,\underline{w}}
(introduced in in
11
) are equal. Thus, for all
m
in the range
0
to
M-2
:

\epsilon_{T}(w_{t_{1}+m},j_{t_{1}+m,\underline{w}})=\epsilon_{T}(w_{t_{2}+m},j_{t_{2}+m,\underline{w}}).

iii)

The indices
j_{t_{1}+M-1,\underline{w}},j_{t_{2}+M-1,\underline{w}}\in\{1,\cdots,h_{w_{t_{1}+M-1}}=h_{w_{t_{2}+M-1}}\}
are different.

Proof.

Item

i)
. Since
\sigma^{t_{1}}(\underline{w})\neq\sigma^{t_{2}}(\underline{w})
, and
\underline{w}
has a period of
P
, there must exist an integer
M\in\{0,\cdots,P-1\}
such that
w_{t_{1}+M}\neq w_{t_{2}+M}
, and
M
is chosen as the smallest integer with this property. We claim that
M\neq 0
because
w_{t_{1}+0}=w_{t_{2}+0}
. Since
M
is the minimum integer where
w_{t_{1}+M}\neq w_{t_{2}+M}
, then for all
0\leq m<M
, it holds that
w_{t_{1}+m}=w_{t_{2}+m}
.

Item

ii)
.If
M=1
, then
w_{t_{1}+1}\neq w_{t_{2}+1}
. Since the incidence matrix
A
is binary, this implies that
j_{t_{1},\underline{w}}\neq j_{t_{2},\underline{w}}
.

Now, let’s consider the case when
M>1
. For each
m\in\{0,\cdots,M-2\}
, the indices
j_{t_{1}+m,\underline{w}}
and
j_{t_{2}+m,\underline{w}}
are the only ones that satisfy
\rho_{T}(w_{t_{1}+m},j_{t_{1}+m,\underline{w}})=(w_{t_{1}+m+1},l_{m})
and
\rho_{T}(w_{t_{2}+m},j_{t_{2}+m,\underline{w}})=(w_{t_{2}+m+1},l^{\prime}_{m})
. However, since
w_{t_{1}+m+1}=w_{t_{2}+m+1}
, it follows that
j_{t_{1}+m,\underline{w}}=j_{t_{2}+m,\underline{w}}
. Consequently, we have:

\epsilon_{T}(w_{t_{1}+m},j_{t_{1}+m,\underline{w}})=\epsilon_{T}(w_{t_{2}+m},j_{t_{2}+m,\underline{w}}).

Item

iii)
. Remember that
\rho_{T}(w_{t_{1}+M-1},j_{t_{1}+M-1,\underline{w}})=(w_{t_{1}+M},l)
and
\rho_{T}(w_{t_{2}+M-1},j_{t_{2}+M-1,\underline{w}})=(w_{t_{2}+M},l^{\prime})
. Since
w_{t_{1}+M}\neq w_{t_{2}+M}
and the incidence matrix of
T
is binary, it is necessarily the case that
j_{t_{1}+M-1,\underline{w}}\neq j_{t_{2}+M-1,\underline{w}}
.
∎

Definition 52
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{G}\mathcal{T}(\textbf{p-A})^{SIM}
and denote its incidence matrix by
A
. Suppose
\underline{w}\in{\mathcal{W}}
is a periodic code of period
P
that is not an
s
-boundary code. Assume that
\sigma^{t_{1}}(\underline{w})\neq\sigma^{t_{2}}(\underline{w})
but
w_{t_{1}}=w_{t_{2}}=i
. Let
M
be the number determined in Lemma
24
. We define the
interchange order
between
(t_{1},\underline{w})
and
(t_{2},\underline{w})
as follows:

•

If
M=1
then
\delta((t_{1},\underline{w}),(t_{2},\underline{w})):=1
.

•

If
M>1
then

\delta((t_{1},\underline{w}),(t_{2},\underline{w}))=\prod_{m=0}^{M-2}\epsilon_{T}(w_{t_{1}+m},j_{t_{1}+m,\underline{w}})=\prod_{m=0}^{M-2}\epsilon_{T}(w_{t_{2}+m},j_{t_{2}+m,\underline{w}}).

Lemma 25
.

Let
T
be a geometric type in the pseudo-Anosov class with a binary incidence matrix
A
. Suppose
\underline{w}\in\Sigma_{A}
is a periodic code of period
P\geq 1
that is not an
s
-boundary code. Assume that
\sigma^{t_{1}}(\underline{w})\neq\sigma^{t_{2}}(\underline{w})
but
w_{t_{1}}=w_{t_{2}}=i
. Consider a realization
(f,{\mathcal{R}})
of the geometric type
T
by a pseudo-Anosov homeomorphism
f:S\to S
. Let
I_{t_{1},\underline{w}}
and
I_{t_{2},\underline{w}}
be the two stable segments determined by the numbers
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
. Then,
I_{t_{1},\underline{w}}<I_{t_{2},\underline{w}}
with respect to the vertical orientation of
R_{i}\in{\mathcal{R}}
if and only if one of the following situations occurs:

•

j_{t_{1}+M-1}<j_{t_{2}+M-1}
and
\delta((t_{1},\underline{w}),(t_{2},\underline{w}))=1
, or

•

j_{t_{1}+M-1}>j_{t_{2}+M-1}
and
\delta((t_{1},\underline{w}),(t_{2},\underline{w}))=-1
,

Proof.

The case when
M=1
reduces to Lemma
23
, so let’s assume that
M>1
. Assume that
I_{t_{1},\underline{w}}<I_{t_{2},\underline{w}}
and call
H
the horizontal sub-rectangle determined by such stable intervals. For all
0\leq m\leq M-2
,
j_{t_{1}+m}=j_{t_{2}+m}
and
f^{m}(H)
is contained in a unique horizontal sub-rectangle of
R_{w_{t_{1}+m}}
. In this case, after applying
f^{M-1}
to
H
, we have two possibilities:

i)

j_{t_{1}+M-1}<j_{t_{2}+M-1}
and
f^{M-1}
preserves the vertical orientation, which happens if and only if
\delta((t_{1},\underline{w}),(t_{2},\underline{w}))=1
, or

ii)

j_{t_{1}+M-1}>j_{t_{2}+M-1}
and
f^{M-1}
changes the vertical orientations, which happens if and only if
\delta((t_{1},\underline{w}),(t_{2},\underline{w}))=-1
.

Now, assume that
j_{t_{1}+M-1}<j_{t_{2}+M-1}
and
\delta((t_{1},\underline{w}),(t_{2},\underline{w}))=1
. Let
H
be the horizontal sub-rectangle of
R_{w_{t_{1}}}
determined by the intervals
I_{t_{1},\underline{w}}
and
I_{t_{2},\underline{w}}
, we need to determine what of such intervals is the upper boundary of
H
.

The number
\delta((t_{1},\underline{w}),(t_{2},\underline{w}))
measures the change in the relative positions of
f^{M-1}(I_{t_{1},\underline{w}})
and
f^{M-1}(I_{t_{2},\underline{w}})
inside the rectangle
R_{w_{t_{1}+M-1}}
. In this case, the relative position of
f^{M-1}(I_{t_{1},\underline{w}})
and
f^{M-1}(I_{t_{2},\underline{w}})
inside
R_{w_{t_{1}+M-1}}
is the same as the relative position of
I_{t_{1}+M-1,\underline{w}}
and
I_{t_{2}+M-1,\underline{w}}
inside
R_{i}
. Since
\delta((t_{1},\underline{w}),(t_{2},\underline{w}))=1
, the upper boundary of
f^{M-1}(H)
coincides with the image of the upper boundary of
H
and similarly, the inferior boundary of
H
corresponds to the inferior boundary of
f^{M-1}(H)
.

Furthermore, since
j_{t_{1}+M-1}<j_{t_{2}+M-1}
and according to Lemma
23
,
I_{t_{1}+M-1,\underline{w}}<I_{t_{2}+M-1,\underline{w}}
. So, we can conclude that the inferior boundary of
f^{M-1}(H)
is contained within
I_{t_{1}+M-1,\underline{w}}
and then the inferior boundary of
H
is
I_{t_{1},\underline{w}}
, and its upper boundary is
I_{t_{2},\underline{w}}
. In this manner, we have
I_{t_{1},\underline{w}}<I_{t_{2},\underline{w}}
with respect to the vertical order in
R_{i}
.

Let’s consider a scenario where
j_{t_{1}+M-1}>j_{t_{2}+M-1}
and
\delta((t_{1},\underline{w}),(t_{2},\underline{w}))=-1
. In this case, we take
H
as the horizontal sub-rectangle of
R_{w_{t_{1}}}
determined by the intervals
I_{t_{1},\underline{w}}
and
I_{t_{2},\underline{w}}
.
The negative value of
\delta((t_{1},\underline{w}),(t_{2},\underline{w}))
indicates that
f^{M-1}|_{H}
alters the relative positions of the stable boundaries of
H
with respect to the vertical order in
R_{i}
. Consequently, the upper boundary of
f^{M-1}(H)
is the image of the inferior boundary of
H
, and simultaneously, the lower boundary of
f^{M-1}(H)
is the image of the upper boundary of
H
.

Given that
j_{t_{1}+M-1}>j_{t_{2}+M-1}
, it follows that the lower boundary of
f^{M-1}(H)
is enclosed within
H^{w_{t_{1}+M-1}}_{j_{t_{1}+M-1,\underline{w}}}
, and the upper boundary is encompassed within
H^{w_{t_{2}+M-1}}_{j_{t_{2}+M-1,\underline{w}}}
. Therefore by Corollary
11
we have that
I_{t_{1}+M-1,\underline{w}}>I_{t_{2}+M-1,\underline{w}}
, and the upper boundary of
f^{M-1}(H)
is confined within
I_{t_{1}+M-1,\underline{w}}
.
Using Lemma
19
, we can deduce that
f^{M-1}(I_{t_{1},\underline{w}})\subset I_{t_{1}+M-1,\underline{w}}
and then that
I_{t_{1},\underline{w}}
is the inferior boundary of
H
. Finally we get that:
I_{t_{1},\underline{w}}<I_{t_{2},\underline{w}}
with respect to the vertical order in
R_{i}
.

∎

We will now establish a formal ordering for the set of stable segments within
R_{i}
, which constitute the stable boundaries of the rectangles contained in
{\mathcal{R}}_{S(\underline{w})}
that also lie within
R_{i}
.

Definition 53
.

Let
R_{i}\in{\mathcal{R}}
be any rectangle, denote
I_{i,-1}:=\partial^{s}_{-1}R_{i}
as the lower boundary of
R_{i}
, and
I_{i,+1}:=\partial^{s}_{+1}R_{i}
as the upper boundary of
R_{i}
. Define:

\underline{s}:{\mathcal{O}}(i,\underline{w})\cup\{(i,+1),(i,-1)\}\rightarrow\{0,1,\cdots,O(i,\underline{w}),O(i,\underline{w})+1\},

as the unique function such that:

•

\underline{s}(t_{1},\underline{w})<\underline{s}(t_{1},\underline{w})
if and only if
I_{t_{1},\underline{w}}<I_{t_{1},\underline{w}}
respect the vertical order in
R_{i}
.

•

It assigns
0
to
(i,-1)
and
O(i,\underline{w})+1
to
(i,+1)
.

The boundaries of any rectangle
\tilde{R}_{r}
of
{\mathcal{R}}_{S(\underline{w})}
contained in
R_{i}
are defined by two consecutive horizontal intervals in
{\mathcal{O}}(i,\underline{w})\cup\{I_{i,-1},I_{i,+1}\}
. The previous order enables us to describe them accurately based on the relative position of
\tilde{R}_{r}
inside
R_{i}
.

Lemma 26
.

Let
\tilde{R}_{r}
be a rectangle in the geometric Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}_{S(\underline{w})}
with
r=\tilde{r}(i,s)
. Then, the stable boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
consists of two stable segments of
R_{i}
determined as follows:

i)

If
s=1
, the lower boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
is
I_{i,-1}
. If
O(i,\underline{w})=0
, then its upper boundary is
I_{i,+1}
. However, if
O(i,\underline{w})>0
, there exists a unique
(t,\underline{w})\in{\mathcal{O}}(i,\underline{w})
such that
\underline{s}(t,\underline{w})=1
, and the upper boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
is the stable segment
I_{t,\underline{w}}
.

ii)

If
O(i,\underline{w})>1
and
s\neq 1,O(i,\underline{w})+1
, there are unique
(t_{1},\underline{w}),(t_{2},\underline{w})\in{\mathcal{O}}(i,\underline{w})
such that:
\underline{s}(t_{1},\underline{w})=s-1
,
\underline{s}(t_{2},\underline{w})=s
. Furthermore, the lower boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
is the stable segment
I_{t_{1},\underline{w}}
, and the upper boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
is the stable segment
I_{t_{2},\underline{w}}
.

iii)

If
O(i,\underline{w})>0
and
s=O(i,\underline{w})+1
, then the upper boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
is the stable segment
I_{i,+1}
. Additionally, there exists a unique
(t,\underline{w})\in{\mathcal{O}}(i,\underline{w})
such that
\underline{s}(t,\underline{w})=O(i,\underline{w})
, and the inferior boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
is the stable segment
I_{t,\underline{w}}
.

Proof.

Recall that
s
is the vertical position of
\tilde{R}_{r}
inside
R_{i}
but
\tilde{s}(t,\underline{w})
is the position of the horizontal interval
I_{t,\underline{w}}
inside
R_{i}
.

Item

i)
. Since
s=1
, within the vertical ordering of
R_{i}
,
\tilde{R}_{r}
represents the first rectangle from
{\mathcal{R}}_{S(\underline{w})}
contained within
R_{i}
. Therefore, its lower boundary coincides with the lower boundary of
R_{i}
, which is
I_{i,-1}
.

If
O(i,\underline{w})=0
, then
\tilde{R}_{r}=R_{i}
, and it’s the only rectangle from
{\mathcal{R}}_{S(\underline{w})}
within
R_{i}
. Consequently, the upper boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
must be
I_{i,+1}
.

However, if
O(i,\underline{w})>0
, the upper boundary of
\tilde{R})_{r}
must correspond to a stable interval
I_{t,\underline{w}}\in{\mathcal{I}}(i,\underline{w})
, which occupies the first position according to the order
\underline{s}
; hence,
\underline{s}(t,\underline{w})=1
.

Item

ii)
. Since
\tilde{R}_{r}
occupies the vertical position
1<s<O(i,\underline{w})+1
among all the rectangles in
{\mathcal{R}}_{S(\underline{w})}
within
R_{i}
, its stable boundaries cannot coincide with
I_{i,-1}
or
I_{i,+1}
. Therefore, its stable boundary is defined by two consecutive stable segments in
{\mathcal{I}}(i,\underline{w})
, denoted as
I_{t_{1},\underline{w}}<I_{t_{2},\underline{w}}
. Furthermore, the interval
I_{t_{1},\underline{w}}
holds the position
s-1
relative to the elements in
{\mathcal{I}}(i,\underline{w})\cup{I_{i,+1},I_{i,-1}}
, and
I_{t_{2},\underline{w}}
is positioned at
s
. This leads to the conclusion that
\underline{s}(t_{1},\underline{w})=s-1
and
\underline{s}(t_{2},\underline{w})=s
.

Item

iii)
.Since
s=O(i,\underline{w})+1
,
\tilde{R}_{r}
is the upper rectangle of
{\mathcal{R}}_{S(\underline{w})}
contained in
R_{i}
. Consequently, its upper boundary is
I_{i,+1}
, and its lower boundary is the segment
I_{t,\underline{w}}
located in position
O(i,\underline{w})
among the elements of
{\mathcal{I}}(i,\underline{w})\cup\{I_{i,+1},I_{i,-1}\}
. Therefore,
\underline{s}(t,\underline{w})=O(i,\underline{w})
.

∎

Let’s determine the image of the stable boundaries of a rectangle
R_{r}
. We are going to use Lemma
19
as our main tool.

Lemma 27
.

Let
\tilde{R}_{r}
, where
r=\tilde{r}(i,s)
, be a rectangle in the geometric Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}_{S(\underline{w})}
. We can determine the image of the horizontal boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
under
f
in the following manner:

i)

If the lower boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
is
I_{i,-1}
and
\Phi_{T}(i,1)=(k,l,\epsilon)
, then
f(I_{i,-1})
is contained within
I_{k,-1}
if
\epsilon=1
, and it is contained within
I_{k,+1}
otherwise.

ii)

If one boundary component of
\tilde{R}_{r}
is the stable segment
I_{t,\underline{w}}
, then
f(I_{t,\underline{w}})\subset I_{t+1,\underline{w}}
.

iii)

If
I_{i,+1}
is the upper boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
and
\Phi_{T}(i,h_{i})=(k,l,\epsilon)
, then
f(I_{i,+1})
is contained in
I_{k,+1}
if
\epsilon=1
and is contained in
I_{k,-1}
otherwise.

Proof.

Item

i)
. It’s evident that the image of
I_{i,-1}
under
f
will be contained either in the upper or lower boundary of
R_{k}
, depending on how
f
alters the vertical orientation. Specifically, if
\epsilon=1
, then
f(I_{i,-1})
is within the lower boundary of
R_{k}
, whereas if
\epsilon=-1
, it will be situated in the upper boundary of
R_{k}
.

Item

ii)
. This is Lemma
19
.

Item

iii)
. The argument is the same as in Item
i)
. ∎

6.3.3.
Generalizations for the family
\mathcal{W}

Almost any result in this sub-section can be proved in the same manner as the respective result was proved for only one code. For example, the next lemma is proved with the same strategy developed in Lemma
24
. The number
M
is the minimum natural number such that
w^{1}_{t+1+M}\neq w^{2}_{t_{2}+M}
, and all the other properties follow from this number.

Lemma 28
.

Suppose that
\underline{w}^{1}
and
\underline{w}^{2}
are two codes in
{\mathcal{W}}
with periods
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
, respectively. Let
t_{1}
be a number in
\{0,\cdots,P_{1}-1\}
and
t_{2}
be a number in
\{0,\cdots,P_{2}-1\}
such that
\sigma^{t_{1}}(\underline{w}^{1})\neq\sigma^{t_{2}}(\underline{w}^{2})
, but both
w^{1}_{t_{1}}
and
w^{2}_{t_{2}}
are equal to
i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
. Consider the two elements in
{\mathcal{O}}(i,{\mathcal{W}})
determined by these iterations:
(t_{1},\underline{w}^{1})
and
(t_{2},\underline{w}^{2}).
In this way:

i)

There exists an integer
M\in\{1,\cdots,P-1\}
such that
w^{1}_{t_{1}+M}\neq w^{2}_{t_{2}+M}
, but for all
0\leq m\leq M-1
, the numbers
w^{1}_{t_{1}+m}
and
w^{2}_{t_{2}+m}
are equal.

ii)

If
M=1
, the indexes
j_{t_{1},\underline{w}^{1}}
and
j_{t_{2},\underline{w}^{2}}
(Definition
20
) are distinct. However, when
M\geq 2
, for every
m
in the set
\{0,\cdots,M-2\}
, the indices
j_{t_{1}+m,\underline{w}^{1}}
and
j_{t_{2}+m,\underline{w}^{1}}
(see Definition
20
) are equal. Thus, for all
m\in\{0,\cdots,M-2\}
:

\epsilon_{T}(w^{1}_{t_{1}+m},j_{t_{1}+m,\underline{w}^{1}})=\epsilon_{T}(w^{2}_{t_{2}+m},j_{t_{2}+m,\underline{w}^{2}}).

iii)

The indices
j_{t_{1}+M-1,\underline{w}^{1}}
and
j_{t_{2}+M-1,\underline{w}^{2}}
in
\{1,\cdots,h_{w^{1}_{t_{1}+M-1}}=h_{w^{2}_{t_{2}+M-1}}\}
are different.

This permits us to define the interchange function as in Definition
52
but this time respect two non necessarily equal codes in
{\mathcal{W}}
.

Definition 54
.

Suppose that
\underline{w}^{1}
and
\underline{w}^{2}
are two codes in
{\mathcal{W}}
with periods
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
, respectively. Let
t_{1}
be a number in
\{0,\cdots,P_{1}-1\}
and
t_{2}
be a number in
\{0,\cdots,P_{2}-1\}
such that
\sigma^{t_{1}}(\underline{w}^{1})\neq\sigma^{t_{2}}(\underline{w}^{2})
but
w^{1}_{t_{1}}=w^{2}_{t_{2}}=i
. Let
M
be the number determined in Lemma
28
. We define the
interchange order
between
(t_{1},\underline{w}^{1})
and
(t_{2},\underline{w}^{2})
as follows:

•

If
M=1
then
\delta((t_{1},\underline{w}^{1}),(t_{2},\underline{w}^{2}))=1
.

•

If
M>1
then

\delta((t_{1},\underline{w}^{1}),(t_{2},\underline{w}^{2}))=\prod_{m=0}^{M-2}\epsilon_{T}(w^{1}_{t_{1}+m},j_{t_{1}+m,\underline{w}^{1}})=\prod_{m=0}^{M-2}\epsilon_{T}(w^{2}_{t_{2}+m},j_{t_{2}+m,\underline{w}^{2}}).

Lemma
25
admits the following generalization whose proof is essentially the same.

Lemma 29
.

Let
I_{t_{1},\underline{w}^{1}}
and
I_{t_{2},\underline{w}^{2}}
be two stable segments such that
w^{1}_{t_{1}}=w^{2}_{t_{2}}=i
. Then,
I_{t_{1},\underline{w}^{1}}<_{v(i)}I_{t_{2},\underline{w}^{2}}
with respect to the vertical orientation of
R_{i}
if and only if one of the following situations occurs:

•

j_{t_{1}+M-1,\underline{w}^{1}}<j_{t_{2}+M-1,\underline{w}^{2}}
and
\delta((t_{1},\underline{w}^{1})(t_{2},\underline{w}^{2}))=1
, or

•

j_{t_{1}+M-1,\underline{w}^{1}}>j_{t_{2}+M-1,\underline{w}^{2}}
and
\delta((t_{1},\underline{w}^{1})(t_{2},\underline{w}^{2}))=-1

Now, we can establish a formal order for the set of stable segments in
R_{i}
that are part of the stable boundary of the rectangles in
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
contained within
R_{i}
.

Definition 55
.

Let
R_{i}\in{\mathcal{R}}
be any rectangle, denote
I_{i,-1}:=\partial^{s}_{-1}R_{i}
as the lower boundary of
R_{i}
, and
I_{i,+1}:=\partial^{s}_{+1}R_{i}
as the upper boundary. Define

\underline{s}:{\mathcal{O}}(i,{\mathcal{W}})\cup\{(i,+1),(i,-1)\}\rightarrow\{0,1,\cdots,O(i,{\mathcal{W}}),O(i,{\mathcal{W}})+1\}.

as the unique function such that:

•

\underline{s}(t_{1},\underline{w}^{1})<\underline{s}(t_{2},\underline{w}^{2})
if and only if
I_{t_{1},\underline{w}^{1}}<I_{t_{1},\underline{w}^{2}}
.

•

It assigns
0
to
(i,-1)
and
O(i,{\mathcal{W}})+1
to
(i,+1)
.

As every rectangle in the partition
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{W})}
that is contained in
R_{i}
is bounded by two consecutive stable segments in
\mathcal{I}(i,\mathcal{W})
, the order function uniquely determines a label for the rectangles in the Markov partition in the following manner:

(1)

It is not difficult to see that the number of rectangles in
\mathcal{R}_{S(\mathcal{W})}
is equal to
O(i,\mathcal{W})+1
.

(2)

Consider all the stable segments contained in
R_{i}
, including their boundaries, ordered vertically in
R_{i}
using the criterion developed in
29
as
\{I_{(i,s)}\}_{s=0}^{O(i,\mathcal{W})+1}
.

(3)

We assign to the rectangle
R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{S(\mathcal{W})}
contained in
R_{i}
, and bounded by the consecutive segments
I_{(i,s)}
and
I_{(i,s+1)}
, with
s\in\{0,\cdots,O(i,\mathcal{W})\}
, the label
R^{\prime}:=R_{(i,s+1)}
for
s\in\{0,\cdots,O(i,\mathcal{W})\}
.

Corollary 12
.

With the labeling of the rectangles in
\mathcal{R}_{S(\mathcal{W})}
described above, the Markov partition
(f,\mathcal{R}_{S(\mathcal{W})})
is a geometric refinement of
(f,\mathcal{R})
by horizontal sub-rectangles.

Definition 56
.

The Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}:=\{\tilde{R}_{r}\}_{r=1}^{N}
with the canonical geometrization is called the
s
-
boundary refinement
of
{\mathcal{R}}
with respect to the family
{\mathcal{W}}
. Its geometric type is denoted by:

(25)

T_{S({\mathcal{W}})}:=\{N,\{H_{r},V_{r}\}_{r=1}^{N},\Phi_{S({\mathcal{W}})}:=(\rho_{S({\mathcal{W}})},\epsilon_{S({\mathcal{W}})})\}.

We label the rectangles in
\mathcal{R}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
using the
lexicographic order

38
as

\tilde{R}_{(i,s)}:=\tilde{R}_{\tilde{r}(i,s)}.

In the following sub-sections, we are going to consider a rectangle
R_{r}
and we are going to assume that we know the terms
(i,s)
such that
r=\tilde{r}(i,s)
. The next lemma indicates how to determine such parameters and justifies our future hypotheses regarding them.

Lemma 30
.

Let
\tilde{R}_{r}
a rectangle in
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
then we can determine two unique numbers
(i_{r},s_{r})
such that
r=\tilde{r}(i_{r},s_{r})
in following manner:

i)

There exist unique
i_{r}\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
such that:

\sum_{i<i_{r}}O(i,{\mathcal{W}})+1<r\leq\sum_{i\leq i_{r}}O(i,{\mathcal{W}})+1

ii)

Take
s_{r}:=r-\sum_{i<i_{r}}O(i,{\mathcal{W}})+1
.

Figure 11.
The set
{\mathcal{I}}(i,\underline{w})
and the rectangles
H^{i}_{j_{t,\underline{w}}}

Proof.

The only point that we need to argue is the first item, as the second one is totally determined by such a number
i_{r}
. But since
r\in\{1,\cdots,\sum_{i=1}^{n}O(i,{\mathcal{W}})+1\}
, in fact, there exists a maximum
i_{0}\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
such that
\sum_{i<i_{0}}O(i,{\mathcal{W}})+1<r
. Clearly
i_{r}=i+1
satisfies the second equality because if this is not the case:
\sum_{i<i_{0}+1}O(i,{\mathcal{W}})+1<r
contradicting that
i_{0}
is the maximum with this property.
∎

6.3.4.
The boundary of the refinement
\mathcal{R}_{S(\mathcal{W})}

Lemma
31
is a generalization of
26
, and Lemma
32
is a generalization of
27
. Their proofs are exactly the same as the results they generalize.

Lemma 31
.

Let
\tilde{R}_{r}
be a rectangle in the geometric Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
with
r=\tilde{r}(i,s)
. Then, the stable boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
consists of two stable segments of
R_{i}
determined as follows:

i)

If
s=1
, the lower boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
is
I_{i,-1}
and if
O(i,{\mathcal{W}})=0
, its upper boundary is
I_{i,+1}
. However, if
O(i,{\mathcal{W}})>0
, there exists a unique
(t,\underline{w}^{q})\in{\mathcal{O}}(i,{\mathcal{W}})
such that
\underline{s}(t,\underline{w}^{q})=1
, and the upper boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
is the stable segment
I_{t,\underline{w}^{q}}
.

ii)

If
O(i,{\mathcal{W}})>1
,
s\neq 1
and
s\neq O(i,{\mathcal{W}})+1
, there are unique
(t_{1},\underline{w}^{1}),(t_{2},\underline{w}^{2})\in{\mathcal{O}}(i,{\mathcal{W}})
such that:
\underline{s}(t_{1},\underline{w}^{1})=s-1
,
\underline{s}(t_{2},\underline{w}^{2})=s
. Furthermore, the lower boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
is the stable segment
I_{t_{1},\underline{w}^{1}}
, and the upper boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
is the stable segment
I_{t_{2},\underline{w}^{2}}
.

iii)

If
O(i,{\mathcal{W}})>0
and
s=O(i,{\mathcal{W}})+1
, then the upper boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
is the stable segment
I_{i,+1}
. Additionally, there exists a unique
(t,\underline{w}^{q})\in{\mathcal{O}}(i,{\mathcal{W}})
such that
\underline{s}(t,\underline{w}^{q})=O(i,{\mathcal{W}})
, and the inferior boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
is the stable segment
I_{t,\underline{w}^{q}}
.

Lemma 32
.

Let
\tilde{R}_{r}
with
r=\tilde{r}(i,s)
be a rectangle in the geometric Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
. Then, the stable boundary components of
\tilde{R}_{r}
have images under
f
determined in the following manner:

i)

If the lower boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
is
I_{i,-1}
and
\Phi_{T}(i,1)=(k,l,\epsilon)
, then: if
\epsilon=1
,
f(I_{i,-1})\subset I_{k,-1}
, but if
\epsilon=-1
,
f(I_{i,-1})\subset I_{k,+1}
.

ii)

If one boundary component of
\tilde{R}_{r}
is the stable segment
I_{t,\underline{w}}
, then
f(I_{t,\underline{w}})\subset I_{t+1,\underline{w}}
.

iii)

If
I_{i,+1}
is the upper boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
and
\Phi_{T}(i,h_{i})=(k,l,\epsilon)
, then: if
\epsilon=1
,
f(I_{i,+1})\subset I_{k,+1}
, but if
\epsilon=-1
,
f(I_{i,+1})\subset I_{k,-1}
.

6.4.
The geometric type of
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{W})}

6.4.1.
The number of rectangles in
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{W})}

The next lemma is immediate.

Lemma 33
.

For all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, the number of rectangles in the Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
that are contained in
R_{i}
is equal to
O(i,{\mathcal{W}})+1
and the number
N
in the geometric type
T_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
, equal to the number of rectangles in the
s
-boundary refinement respect to
{\mathcal{W}}
, is determined by the following formula:

(26)

N=\sum_{i=1}^{n}O(i,{\mathcal{W}})+1.

6.4.2.
The vertical sub-rectangles in
T_{\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{W})}

Definition 57
.

Let
\tilde{R}_{r}
be a rectangle in the geometric Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
and suppose that
r=\tilde{r}(i,s)
. Let
V^{i}_{l}
be a vertical sub-rectangle of the Markov partition
(f,{\mathcal{R}})
contained in
R_{i}
. Define:

\tilde{V}^{r}_{l}:=\overline{\overset{o}{\tilde{R}_{r}}\cap\overset{o}{V^{i}_{l}}}.

as the closure of the intersection between the interior of
\tilde{R}_{r}
and the interior of
V^{i}_{l}
.

Lemma 34
.

The set
\tilde{V}^{r}_{l}
is a vertical sub-rectangle of
\tilde{R}_{r}

Proof.

The intersection of the interiors of a horizontal and a vertical sub-rectangle of
R_{i}
has a unique connected component. In particular,
\overset{o}{V^{i}_{l}}\cap\overset{o}{\tilde{R}_{r}}
has a unique connected component. Clearly,
V^{i}_{l}
crosses
\tilde{R}_{r}
from the bottom to the top, and therefore,
\tilde{V}^{r}_{l}
is a vertical sub-rectangle of
\tilde{R}_{r}
.
∎

The next is just a technical lemme to be used more late.

Lemma 35
.

Let
\tilde{R}_{r}\in\mathcal{R}_{S(\mathcal{W})}
with
r=\tilde{r}(i,s)
. Let
\tilde{H}\subset\tilde{R}_{r}
be a horizontal sub-rectangle of
(f,\mathcal{R}_{S(\mathcal{W})})
. Then there is a unique pair
(i,j)\in\mathcal{H}(T)
such that
\overset{o}{\tilde{H}}\subset\overset{o}{H^{i}_{j}}
, where
H^{i}_{j}
is a horizontal sub-rectangle of
(f,\mathcal{R})
.

Proof.

We claim that
\tilde{H}
intersects a unique horizontal sub-rectangle
H^{i}_{j}
in its interior. If this is not the case, there are two adjacent sub-rectangles of
R_{i}
,
H^{i}_{j_{1}}
and
H^{i}_{j_{2}}
, sharing a stable boundary
I
that lies inside the interior of
\tilde{H}
, i.e.,
\overset{o}{I}\subset\overset{o}{\tilde{H}}
. Then,
f(\overset{o}{\tilde{H}})
will intersect the stable boundary of
\mathcal{R}
in the stable interval
f(\overset{o}{I})
. However, since the stable boundary of
\mathcal{R}
is contained in the stable boundary of
\mathcal{R}_{S(\mathcal{W})}
, the interval
f(\overset{o}{I})
is contained in the stable boundary of
\mathcal{R}_{S(\mathcal{W})}
, leading to a contradiction. Therefore,
\overset{o}{\tilde{H}}
is contained in the interior of a unique horizontal sub-rectangle
H^{i}_{j}
.
∎

Lemma 36
.

The rectangles
\{\tilde{V}^{r}_{l}\}_{l=1}^{v_{i}}
introduced in Definition
57
are the vertical sub-rectangles of the Markov partition
(f,\mathcal{R}_{S(\mathcal{W})})
that are contained in
\tilde{R}_{r}
.

Proof.

Consider a single rectangle
\tilde{V}^{r}_{l}
. We can observe that
f^{-1}(\tilde{V}^{r}_{l})
is a subset of
f^{-1}(V^{k}_{l})
, and by Lemma
35
, it is contained in some horizontal sub-rectangle of
\mathcal{R}
,
H^{i}_{j}
. The preimage
f^{-1}(\tilde{V}^{r}_{l})
is a horizontal sub-rectangle of
H^{i}_{j}
whose interior does not intersect
\partial^{s}\mathcal{R}_{S(\mathcal{W})}
, and it is exclusively contained within a unique rectangle
\tilde{R}_{r}
. Moreover, the stable boundary of
f^{-1}(\tilde{V}^{r}_{l})
has an image contained within the stable boundary of the Markov partition
\mathcal{R}_{S(\mathcal{W})}
. Therefore,
f^{-1}(\tilde{V}^{r}_{l})
is a horizontal sub-rectangle of
\mathcal{R}_{S(\mathcal{W})}
, and hence
\tilde{V}^{r}_{l}
is a vertical sub-rectangle of the Markov partition
\mathcal{R}_{S(\mathcal{W})}
.

Conversely, if
\tilde{V}
is a vertical sub-rectangle of
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
, then
f^{-1}(\tilde{V})=\tilde{H}
is a horizontal sub-rectangle of
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
. Let
(i,j_{\tilde{H}})
be the indexes give in Definition
35
. Thus,
\tilde{V}
is a horizontal sub-rectangle of
V^{k}_{l}=f(H^{i}_{j_{\tilde{H}}})
, and its interior does not intersect the stable boundaries of
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
. However, its stable boundaries are contained in
\partial^{s}{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
. This implies that
\tilde{V}\subset V^{k}_{l}\cap\tilde{R}_{r}
for some
r\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
, and it turns out to be one of the rectangles
\tilde{V}^{r}_{l}
given by
\tilde{V}^{r}_{l}:=\overline{\overset{o}{\tilde{R}_{r}}\cap\overset{o}{V^{k}_{l}}}
.

∎

This result have the next immediate corollary.

Corollary 13
.

If
r=\tilde{r}(i,s)
then
V_{r}=v_{i}
.

Corollary 14
.

Let
\tilde{H}\subset\tilde{R}_{r}
any horizontal sub-rectangle of
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}}}
, assume that
\tilde{H}\subset H^{i}_{j}
and
f(H^{i}_{j})=V^{k}_{l}
. If
f(H)=\tilde{V}^{r^{\prime}}_{l^{\prime}}
then
l=l^{\prime}
.

Proof.

As
\tilde{H}\subset H^{i}_{j}
, then
f(\tilde{H})\subset f(H^{i}_{j})=V^{k}_{l}
. Simultaneously,
f(H)=\tilde{V}^{r^{\prime}}_{l^{\prime}}=\overline{\overset{o}{\tilde{R}_{r^{\prime}}}\cap\overset{o}{V^{k}_{l}}}
. Given that the intersection
\overset{o}{\tilde{R}_{r^{\prime}}}\cap\overset{o}{V^{k}_{l}}
has only one connected component, it is necessary that
\overset{o}{V^{k}_{l}}=\overset{o}{V^{k}_{l^{\prime}}}
and thus
l=l^{\prime}
.
∎

6.4.3.
The relative position of
H^{i}_{j}
with respect to
\tilde{R}_{r}

We are going to determine the set of horizontal sub-rectangles of
\mathcal{R}
that intersect a certain rectangle
R_{r}
from
\mathcal{R}_{S(\mathcal{W})}
. For this reason, let us introduce the set that indexes such sub-rectangles.

Definition 58
.

Let
\tilde{R}_{r}\in{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
with
r=\tilde{r}(i,s)
. We define:

{\mathcal{H}}(r):=\{(i,j)\in{\mathcal{H}}(T):\overset{o}{H^{i}_{j}}\cap\overset{o}{\tilde{R}_{r}}\neq\emptyset\}.

If we know the stable segments that bound
\tilde{R}_{r}
, we can determine the indices of the horizontal sub-rectangles within
{\mathcal{R}}
that contain the upper and lower boundaries of
\tilde{R}_{r}
. Let’s assume these horizontal sub-rectangles are in positions
j{-}<j{+}
. Thus, we can define
{\mathcal{H}}(r)
as the set of indices
(i,j)
where
j
ranges from
j_{-}
to
j_{+}
. The following lemma provides a more detailed explanation of this observation.

Figure 12.
The relative position of the horizontal sub-rectangles inside
\tilde{R}_{r}

Lemma 37
.

Let
\tilde{R}_{r}
be a rectangle in the geometric Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}_{S(\underline{w})}
with
r=\tilde{r}(i,s)
and
s\in{0,\cdots,O(i,{\mathcal{W}})}
. Then the set
{\mathcal{H}}(r)
is determined by one of the three following formulas:

i)

If
O(i,{\mathcal{W}})=0
, then:
{\mathcal{H}}(r)=\{(i,j)\in{\mathcal{H}}(T)\}
.

ii)

If
O(i,{\mathcal{W}})>0
,
s\neq 0
and
s\neq O(i,{\mathcal{W}})
, let
I_{t_{1},\underline{w}^{1}}
be the inferior boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
, and let
I_{t_{2},\underline{w}^{2}}
be the upper boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
. Let
j_{t_{1},\underline{w}^{1}}
and
j_{t_{1},\underline{w}^{2}}
be the indices determined in Lemma
22
. In this situation:

{\mathcal{H}}(r)=\{(i,j)\in{\mathcal{H}}(T):j_{t_{1},\underline{w}^{1}}\leq j\leq j_{t_{2},\underline{w}^{2}}\}.

iii)

If
O(i,{\mathcal{W}})>0
and
s=O(i,{\mathcal{W}})
, let
I_{t_{1},\underline{w}^{1}}
be the inferior boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
and
I_{i,+1}
the upper boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
. Let
j_{t_{1},\underline{w}^{1}}
be the index determined in
22
. In this manner:

{\mathcal{H}}(r)=\{(i,j)\in{\mathcal{H}}(T):j_{t_{1},\underline{w}^{1}}\leq j\leq h_{i}\}.

iv)

If
O(i,{\mathcal{W}})>0
and
s=0
, let
I_{t_{1},\underline{w}^{1}}
be the upper boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
, let
I_{i,-1}
be its inferior boundary and let
j_{t,\underline{w}^{1}}
be the index determined in
22
. In this manner:

{\mathcal{H}}(r)=\{(i,j)\in{\mathcal{H}}(T):1\leq j\leq j_{t_{1},\underline{w}^{1}}\}.

Proof.

Item

i)
. If
O(i,{\mathcal{W}})=0
, then
\tilde{R}_{r}=R_{i}
, indicating that
{\mathcal{H}}(r)
must include the labels of all the horizontal sub-rectangles within the Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}
that are contained in
R_{i}
. Thus,
{\mathcal{H}}(r)=\{(i,j)\in{\mathcal{H}}(T)\}
.

Item

ii)
. The lower boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
lies within
H^{i}_{j_{t_{1},\underline{w}^{1}}}
, while the upper boundary is contained in
H^{i}_{j_{t_{2},\underline{w}^{2}}}
. It is evident that all the horizontal sub-rectangles of the form
H^{i}_{j}
with
j_{t_{1},\underline{w}}\leq j\leq j_{t_{2},\underline{w}}
are the only ones that satisfy
\overset{\circ}{H^{i}_{j}}\cap\overset{\circ}{\tilde{R}_{r}}\neq\emptyset
. This implies that
\mathcal{H}(r)=\{(i,j)\in\mathcal{H}(T)\mid j_{t_{1},\underline{w}}\leq j\leq j_{t_{2},\underline{w}}\}
.

Item

iii)
. Every horizontal sub-rectangle
H^{i}_{j}
with
j_{t_{1},\underline{w}}\leq j\leq\leq h_{i}
satisfies that
\overset{o}{H^{i}_{j}}\cap\overset{o}{\tilde{R}_{r}}\neq\emptyset
, and these are the only horizontal sub-rectangles within
{\mathcal{R}}
with this property. Thus,
{\mathcal{H}}(r)=\{(i,j)\in{\mathcal{H}}(T):j_{t,\underline{w}}\leq j\leq h_{i}\}
.

Item

iv)
. The argument is the same as in Item
iii)
.
∎

6.4.4.
The number of horizontal sub-rectangles of
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}

We are going to count how many rectangles in the Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
are contained in the intersection
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j}
and describe the way that
f
send such horizontal sub-rectangles into vertical sub-rectangles.

Fist case:

\tilde{R}_{r}\subset H^{i}_{j}\setminus\partial^{s}H^{i}_{j}

Figure 13.
First case:
\tilde{R}_{r}\subset H^{i}_{j}\setminus\partial^{s}H^{i}_{j}

Lemma 38
.

Let
\tilde{R}_{r}\in{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
, with
r=\tilde{r}(i,s)
. Suppose we have a pair of indices
(i,j)\in{\mathcal{H}}(r)
such that
\tilde{R}_{r}\subset H^{i}_{j}\setminus\partial^{s}H^{i}_{j}
. In this case
s\neq
and
s\neq O(i,{\mathcal{W}})
and we take
I_{t_{1},\underline{w}^{1}}
as the lower boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
and
I_{t_{2},\underline{w}^{2}}
as the upper boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
.

Assume that
\rho_{T}(i,j)=(k,l)
. Let
s^{-}=\underline{s}(t_{1}+1,\underline{w}^{1})
and
s^{+}=\underline{s}(t_{2}+1,\underline{w}^{2})
. Then,
w^{1}_{t_{1}+1}=w^{2}_{t_{2}+1}=k
and the number of horizontal sub-rectangles of
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
contained in the intersection
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j}
is given by the formula:

(27)

\underline{h}(r,j)=|s^{-}-s^{+}|.

Proof.

Look at Figure
13
. Since
\tilde{R}_{r}\subset H^{i}_{j}\setminus\partial^{s}H^{i}_{j}
,
\tilde{R}_{r}
doesn’t share a stable boundary component with
R_{i}
. This means
s\neq 0
and
s\neq O(i,\mathcal{W})
, and we can take the intervals
I_{t_{1},\underline{w}^{1}}
and
I_{t_{2},\underline{w}^{2}}
as described in the lemma.

Since
f(\tilde{R}_{r})\subset f(H^{i}_{j})\subset R_{k}
, then
w_{t_{1}+1}^{1}=w_{t_{2}+1}^{2}=k
, as we claimed. The number of horizontal sub-rectangles of
\tilde{R}_{r}
is equal to the number of all the rectangles in
\mathcal{R}_{S(\mathcal{W})}
contained in
R_{k}
and located between the intervals
I_{t_{1}+1,\underline{w}^{1}}
and
I_{t_{2}+2,\underline{w}^{2}}
. If their relative positions are given by the numbers
s^{-}
and
s^{+}
, then the number of such rectangles is given by
|s^{-}-s^{+}|
, and we have proved our lemma.
∎

Definition 59
.

The horizontal sub-rectangles of
\tilde{R}_{r}
as described in Lemma
38
are labeled from the bottom to the top with respect to the orientation of
\tilde{R}_{r}
as:

\{\tilde{H}^{r}_{j,J}\}_{J=1}^{\underline{h}(r,j)}.

Lemma 39
.

With the hypothesis and notations of Lemma
38
, assume that
(\rho,\epsilon)(i,j)=(k,l,\epsilon(i,j))
and
f(\tilde{H}^{r}_{j,J})=\tilde{V}^{r^{\prime}}_{l^{\prime}}
. Then:

i)

If
\epsilon=\epsilon(i,j)=1
, then
s^{-}<s^{+}
,
r^{\prime}=\tilde{r}(k,s^{-}+J)
and
l=l^{\prime}
.

ii)

If
\epsilon=\epsilon(i,j)=-1
, then
s^{-}>s^{+}
,
r^{\prime}=\tilde{r}(k,s^{-}-J+1)
and
l=l^{\prime}
.

where
\underline{s}:{\mathcal{O}}(i,{\mathcal{W}})\cup\{(i,+1),(i,-1)\}\rightarrow\{0,1,\cdots,O(i,{\mathcal{W}}),O(i,{\mathcal{W}})+1\}.
is the order function that was introduced in Lemma
55
.

Proof.

Item

i)
. If
\epsilon(i,j)=1
then
f
preserves the vertical orientation of
\tilde{R}_{r}
, and then
I_{t_{1}+1,\underline{w}^{1}}<I_{t_{2}+1,\underline{w}^{2}}
, so
s^{-}<s^{+}
.

The rectangle
\tilde{H}^{r}_{j,J}
is in vertical position
J
inside
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j}
and is mapped by
f
to the rectangle whose vertical position inside
R_{k}
is
J
positions above the rectangle in position
s^{-}
within
R_{k}
. Therefore,
f(\tilde{H}^{r}_{j,J})
is inside a rectangle
\tilde{R}_{r^{\prime}}\subset R_{k}
whose position is equal to
s^{-}+J
. In other words,
r^{\prime}=\tilde{r}(k,s^{-}+J)
.

Item

ii)
. If
\epsilon(i,j)=-1
then
f
inverts the vertical orientation of
\tilde{R}_{r}
. Then,
I_{t_{1}+1,\underline{w}^{1}}>I_{t_{2}+1,\underline{w}^{2}}
and
s^{-}>s^{+}
.

The rectangle
\tilde{H}^{r}_{j,1}
that is positioned at place
1
inside
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j}
is sent into to the rectangle
\tilde{R}_{r^{\prime}}
, whose upper boundary is at position
s^{-}
, therefore
r^{\prime}=s^{-}=s^{-}-(J-1)
. In general the upper boundary of the rectangles
\tilde{R}\subset R_{k}
determine its position inside
R_{k}
. That means that
f(\tilde{H}^{r}_{j,J})
is inside a rectangle
\tilde{R}_{r^{\prime}}
withing
R_{k}
whose position is given by
s^{-}-J+1
. In other words,
r^{\prime}=\tilde{r}(k,s^{-}-J+1)
.

Finally in bot cases, Corollary
14
implies that
l^{\prime}=l
.
∎

This lets us to introduce the following definition.

Definition 60
.

With the hypothesis of Lemma
38
, assume that
\Phi_{T}(i,j)=(k,l,\epsilon)
. Then:

If
\epsilon(i,j)=1
we define:

(28)

\underline{\rho}_{(r,j)}(r,J)=(\tilde{r}(k,s^{-}+J),l).

and if
\epsilon(i,j)=-1
:

(29)

\underline{\rho}_{(r,j)}(r,J)=(\tilde{r}(k,s^{+}-J+1),l).

Second case:
H^{i}_{j}\subset\tilde{R}_{r}

Figure 14.
Second case:
H^{i}_{j}\subset\tilde{R}_{r}

Lemma 40
.

Let
\tilde{R}_{r}\in{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
, with
r=\tilde{r}(i,s)
. Let
(i,j)\in{\mathcal{H}}(r)
be a pair of indices such that
H^{i}_{j}\subset\tilde{R}_{r}
, assume that
\rho_{T}(i,j)=(k,l)
, then the number of horizontal sub-rectangles of
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
that are contained in the intersection
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j}
is given by the formula:

(30)

\underline{h}(r,j)=O(k,{\mathcal{W}})+1,

Proof.

The image of
f^{(}H^{i}_{j}\cap\tilde{R}_{r})=f(H^{i}_{j})=V^{k}_{l}
and
V^{k}_{l}
intersect all the rectangles of
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
that are contained in
R_{k}
, the total amount of them is
O(k,{\mathcal{W}})+1,
and this is the number of horizontal sub-rectangles of
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
that are contained in
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j}
.
∎

Definition 61
.

The horizontal sub-rectangles of
\tilde{R}_{r}
described in Lemma
40
are labeled from bottom to top with respect to the vertical orientation of
\tilde{R}_{r}
as:

\{\tilde{H}^{r}_{j,J}\}_{J=1}^{\underline{h}(r,j)}.

Now we can determine the image of any horizontal sub-rectangle.

Lemma 41
.

Let
\tilde{R}_{r}\in{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
, with
r=\tilde{r}(i,s)
. Let
(i,j)\in{\mathcal{H}}(r)
be a pair of indices such that
H^{i}_{j}\subset\tilde{R}_{r}
, and let
\tilde{H}^{r}_{(j,J)}
be an horizontal sub-rectangle of
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j}
. Assume that
\rho_{T}(i,j)=(k,l)
, and that
f(\tilde{H}^{r}_{j,J})=\tilde{V}^{r^{\prime}}_{l^{\prime}}
, then:

i)

If
\epsilon(i,j)=1
, then
r^{\prime}=\tilde{r}(k,J)
and
l=l^{\prime}
.

ii)

If
\epsilon_{T}(i,j)=-1
, then
r^{\prime}=\tilde{r}(k,O(k,{\mathcal{W}})+1-J+1)
and
l=l^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Item

i)
. If
\epsilon(i,j)=1
, the vertical orientation is preserved by
f
restricted to
\tilde{R}_{r}
, and the horizontal sub-rectangle
\tilde{H}^{r}_{j,J}
that is in position

inside

is sent to the rectangle

located in position
J
inside
R_{k}
. This means that
r^{\prime}=\tilde{r}(k,J)
and in view of Corollary
14
,
l^{\prime}=l
.

Item

ii)
. If
\epsilon(i,j)=-1
, the vertical orientation is invert by
f
restricted to
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j}
, and the horizontal sub-rectangle
\tilde{H}^{r}_{j,J}
that is in position
J
inside
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j}
is sent to the rectangle
\tilde{R}_{r^{\prime}}
located in position
J-1
below the top rectangle of
{\mathcal{R}}_{S(\underline{w})}
(for
J=1
the position is
O(k,\underline{w}))+1)
, and this position is equal to
O(k,\underline{w})+1-J+1
inside
R_{k}
. This means that
r^{\prime}=\tilde{r}(k,O(k,\underline{w})+1-J+1)
. Finally, by Corollary
14
,
l^{\prime}=l
.

∎

Definition 62
.

Let
\tilde{R}_{r}\in{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
, with
r=\tilde{r}(i,s)
. Let
(i,j)\in{\mathcal{H}}(r)
be a pair of indices such that

, and let

. Assume that
\rho_{T}(i,j)=(k,l)
, then:

we define:

\underline{\rho}_{(r,j)}(r,J)=(\tilde{r}(k,J),l),

and if

Third case:

H^{i}_{j}
contains only one horizontal boundary component of
\tilde{R}_{r}
.

Figure 15.
H^{i}_{j}
contains only one horizontal boundary component of
\tilde{R}_{r}

Lemma 42
.

Consider
\tilde{R}_{r}\in{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
, where
r=\tilde{r}(i,s)
, and assume that
O(i,{\mathcal{W}})>0
. Take a pair of indexes
(i,j)\in{\mathcal{H}}(r)
such that
H^{i}_{j}
exclusively contains the upper boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
. Let
I_{t,\underline{w}^{1}}
(with
\underline{w}^{1}\in{\mathcal{W}}
) be the upper boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
. Let
s^{+}=\underline{s}(t+1,\underline{w}^{1})
and assume that
\rho_{T}(i,j)=(k,l)
. Then, the number of horizontal sub-rectangles of
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
that are contained in
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j}
can be computed using one of the following formulas:

•

If
\epsilon_{T}(i,j)=1
:

(33)

\underline{h}(r,j)=s^{+}

•

If
\epsilon_{T}(i,j)=-1

(34)

\underline{h}(r,j)=O(k,{\mathcal{W}})+1-s^{+}.

Look the Figure
15
to get some pictorial intuition. about our arguments

Proof.

Case

\epsilon_{T}(i,j)=1
. The upper boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
is mapped to the stable segment
I_{t+1,\underline{w}^{1}}
, positioned at place
s^{+}
inside
R_{k}
and the inferior boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j}
coincides with the inferior boundary of
H^{i}_{j}
and since
\epsilon_{T}(i,j)=1
,
f
preserves the vertical orientation of
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j}
. Consequently, the image of the inferior boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j}
is contained in the inferior boundary of
R_{k}
. The number of rectangles in
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
between the segment
I_{t+1,\underline{w}^{1}}
and the inferior boundary of
R_{k}
is given by
s^{+}
and this number is equal to the number of horizontal sub-rectangles of
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j}
.

Case

\epsilon_{T}(i,j)=-1
. The upper boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j}
is equal to the upper boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
and is mapped by
f
to the stable segment
I_{t+1,\underline{w}^{1}}
, positioned at place
s^{+}
inside
R_{k}
. The inferior boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j}
coincides with the inferior boundary of
H^{i}_{j}
, but since
\epsilon_{T}(i,j)=-1
,
f
inverts the vertical orientation of
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j}
. Consequently, the image of the inferior boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j}
is contained in the upper boundary of
R_{k}
. The number of rectangles in
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
between the segment
I_{t+1,\underline{w}}
and the upper boundary of
R_{k}
is given by
O(k,{\mathcal{W}})+1-s^{+}
and this number is equal to the number of horizontal sub-rectangles of
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j}
.

∎

The next lemma addresses the situation in which the rectangle
H^{i}_{j}
just contains the inferior boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
. Its proof follows a similar approach to that of Lemma
42
, so we won’t delve into further details.

Lemma 43
.

Consider
\tilde{R}_{r}\in{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
, where
r=\tilde{r}(i,s)
, and assume that
O(i,{\mathcal{W}})>0
. Take a pair of indexes
(i,j)\in{\mathcal{H}}(r)
such that
H^{i}_{j}
just contains the lower boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
. Let
I_{t,\underline{w}^{1}}
(with
\underline{w}^{1}\in{\mathcal{W}}
) be the lower boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
, and denote
s^{+}=\underline{s}(t+1,\underline{w}^{1})
. In this scenario, the number of horizontal sub-rectangles of
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
that are contained in
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j}
can be determined using one of the following formulas:

•

If
\epsilon_{T}(i,j)=1
:

(35)

\underline{h}(r,j)=O(k,{\mathcal{W}})+1-s^{+}

•

If
\epsilon_{T}(i,j)=-1

(36)

\underline{h}(r,j)=s^{+}.

Definition 63
.

The horizontal sub-rectangles of
\tilde{R}_{r}
described in Lemma
42
and Lemma
43
are labeled from the bottom to the top with the vertical orientation of
\tilde{R}_{r}
as:

\{\tilde{H}^{r}_{j,J}\}_{J=1}^{\underline{h}(r,j)}.

Lemma 44
.

With the hypothesis of Lemma
42
, suppose that
H^{i}_{j}
just contains the upper boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
and assume that
\Phi_{T}(i,j)=(k,l,\epsilon_{T}(i,j))
and
f(\tilde{H}^{r}_{j,J})=\tilde{V}^{r^{\prime}}_{l^{\prime}}
. Then we have the following situations:

i)

If
\epsilon_{T}(i,j)=1
, then
r^{\prime}=\tilde{r}(k,J)
and
l=l^{\prime}

ii)

If
\epsilon_{T}(i,j)=-1
, then
r^{\prime}=\tilde{r}(k,O(k,{\mathcal{W}})+1-J+1)
and
l=l^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Item

i)
. When
\epsilon_{T}(i,j)=1
,
f
preserves the vertical orientation restricted to
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j}
. Consequently, the horizontal sub-rectangle
\tilde{H}^{r}_{j,J}
that is located at position
J
within
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j}
, is send to the rectangle
\tilde{R}_{r^{\prime}}
that is located at position
J
over the rectangle that is in the position
s^{+}
within
R_{k}
, therefore
r^{\prime}=\tilde{r}(k,s^{+}+J)
.

Item

ii)
. When
\epsilon_{T}(i,j)=-1
,
f
inverts the vertical orientation of
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j}
. Consequently, the horizontal sub-rectangle
\tilde{H}^{r}_{j,J}
that is located at position
J
within
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j}
, is send to the rectangle located at position
J-1
below the upper rectangle in
R_{k}
, this is given by
O(k,\underline{w})+1-(J-1)
withing
R_{k}
. Therefore,
r^{\prime}=\tilde{r}(k,O(k,\underline{w})+1-J+1)
.

In each case Corollary
14
implies that
l^{\prime}=l
.

∎

A similar statement is true when the rectangle
H^{i}_{j}
contains only the lower boundary of
\tilde{R}_{r}
.

Lemma 45
.

With the hypothesis of Lemma

, suppose that

and assume that
\Phi_{T}(i,j)=(k,l,\epsilon_{T}(i,j))
and
f(\tilde{H}^{r}_{j,J})=\tilde{V}^{r^{\prime}}_{l^{\prime}}
. Then we have the following situations:

i)

If
\epsilon_{T}(i,j)=1
, then
r^{\prime}=\tilde{r}(k,s^{+}+J)
and
l=l^{\prime}

ii)

If

and
l=l^{\prime}
.

6.4.5.
Collecting the information

Now, we can collect the information from the three cases to compute the number of horizontal sub-rectangles in
\tilde{R}_{r}
.

Corollary 15
.

Let
\tilde{R}_{r}\in{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
with
r=\tilde{r}(i,s)
, then the number
H_{r}
of horizontal sub-rectangles of the Markov partition
(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})})
that are contained in
\tilde{R}_{r}
is given by:

(37)

H_{r}=\sum_{\{j:(i,j)\in{\mathcal{H}}(r)\}}\underline{h}(r,j).

The function
\rho_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
in the geometric type
T_{S({\mathcal{W}})}

Definition 64
.

The sub-rectangles of the geometric Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
contained in
\tilde{R}_{r}
are labeled as:

\{H^{r}_{\underline{J}}\}_{\underline{J}=1}^{H_{r}}

from the bottom to the top with respect to the vertical orientation of
\tilde{R}_{r}
.

The next lemma is immediate.

Lemma 46
.

For each
\underline{J}\in\{1,\cdots,H_{r}\}
, there exists a unique index
j_{(\underline{J})}
such that
(i,j)\in{\mathcal{H}}(r)
and
\tilde{H}^{r}_{\underline{J}}\subset H^{i}_{j}\cap\tilde{R}_{r}
. The index
j_{(\underline{J})}
is determined by satisfy the next inequalities:

(38)

\sum_{\{j^{\prime}:(i,j^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{H}}(r)\text{ and }j^{\prime}<j_{(\underline{J})}\}}\underline{h}(j^{\prime},r)<\underline{J}\leq\sum_{\{j^{\prime}:(i,j^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{H}}(r)\text{ and }j^{\prime}\leq j_{(\underline{J})}\}}\underline{h}(j^{\prime},r).

Definition 65
.

Given
\underline{J}\in\{1,\cdots,H_{r}\}
we call the index
j_{(\underline{J})}
that was determined in Lemma
46
the
horizontal parameter
of
\underline{J}
.

Definition 66
.

Let
r=\tilde{r}(i,s)
,
\underline{J}\in\{1,\cdots,H_{r}\}
, and let
j_{(\underline{J})}
be the horizontal parameter of
\underline{J}
. The
relative index
of
H^{r}_{J}
inside of
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j_{(\underline{J}})}
is given by:

(39)

J_{(\underline{J})}:=\underline{J}-\sum_{j^{\prime}<j_{({\underline{J})}}}\underline{h}(r,j^{\prime}).

Definition 67
.

Let
\underline{J}\in\{1,\cdots,H_{r}\}
the
relative position
of
\underline{J}
inside
\tilde{R}_{r}
is the pair
(j_{\underline{J}},J_{(\underline{J})})
of the horizontal parameter of
\underline{J}
and the relative index of
H^{r}_{J}
inside of
\tilde{R}_{r}\cap H^{i}_{j_{\underline{J}}}

Lemma 47
.

The rectangle
\tilde{H}^{r}_{j_{(\underline{J})},J_{(\underline{J})}}
and the rectangle
\tilde{H}^{r}_{\underline{J}}
are the same.

Corollary 16
.

Let
r=\tilde{r}(i,s)
and
\underline{J}\in\{1,\cdots,H_{r}\}
. Let
(j_{(\underline{J})},J_{(\underline{J})})
the relative position of
\underline{J}
as was given in Definition

. Then the function
\rho_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
is given by the formula:

(40)

\rho_{S({\mathcal{W}})}(r,\underline{J}):=\underline{\rho}_{(r,j_{(\underline{J})})}(r,J_{(\underline{J})}).

The orientation
\epsilon_{S({\mathcal{W}})}

Lemma 48
.

Let
r=\tilde{r}(i,s)
and
\underline{J}\in\{1,\cdots,H_{r}\}
. Let
j_{(\underline{J})}
the horizontal parameter of
\underline{J}
(Definition
