). Then:

(41)

\epsilon_{S(\underline{w})}(r,\underline{J}):=\epsilon_{T}(i,j_{(\underline{J})}).

We can summarize our discussion in the following corollary.

Corollary 17
.

Let
T
be a geometric type in the pseudo-Anosov class with a binary incidence matrix denoted as
A:=A(T)
. Let
{\mathcal{W}}
a family of periodic codes contained in
\Sigma_{A}
, there is algorithm to compute the geometric type:

T_{S({\mathcal{W}})}:=\{N,\{H_{r},V_{r}\}_{r=1}^{N},\Phi_{S({\mathcal{W}})}:=(\rho_{S({\mathcal{W}})},\epsilon_{S({\mathcal{W}})})\}.

using the given geometric type
T
and the codes in
{\mathcal{W}}
.

We claim that Theorem
7
has been essentially proved, so let us recapitulate our results.

Theorem

(
7
)
.

Given a geometric partition
\mathcal{R}=\{R_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
of
f
, the geometric type
T
of the pair
(f,\mathcal{R})
, and a finite family of periodic codes
{\mathcal{W}}=\{\underline{w}^{1},\cdots,\underline{w}^{Q}\}\subset\Sigma_{A(T)}
, there exists a finite algorithm to construct a geometric refinement of
{\mathcal{R}}
by horizontal sub-rectangles of the pair
(f,\mathcal{R})
, known as the
s
-
boundary
refinement of
(f,\mathcal{R})
with respect to
{\mathcal{W}}
, denoted by
(f,\textbf{S}_{{\mathcal{W}}}(\mathcal{R}))
, with the following properties:

•

Every rectangle in the Markov partition
\textbf{S}_{{\mathcal{W}}}(\mathcal{R})
has as stable boundary components either a stable boundary arc of a rectangle
R_{i}
in
\mathcal{R}
or a stable arc
I_{t,\underline{w}}
determined by the iteration
t
of a code
\underline{w}\in{\mathcal{W}}
. Moreover, every arc of the form
I_{t,\underline{w}}
for
\underline{w}\in{\mathcal{W}}
is the stable boundary component of some rectangle in the refinement
\textbf{S}_{{\mathcal{W}}}(\mathcal{R})
.

•

The periodic boundary points of
\textbf{S}_{{\mathcal{W}}}(\mathcal{R})
are the union of the periodic boundary points of
\mathcal{R}
and those in the set
\{\pi_{(f,{\mathcal{R}})}(\sigma_{A}^{t}(\underline{w}))\mid\underline{w}\in{\mathcal{W}}\text{ and }t\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
.

•

There are explicit formulas, in terms of the geometric type
T
and the codes in
{\mathcal{W}}
, to compute the geometric type of
\textbf{S}_{{\mathcal{W}}}(\mathcal{R})
.

Proof.

We have constructed
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{W})}
in Lemma
50
, and it was done in such a manner that the rectangles obtained satisfy the first item of our proposition. Since the projection of the codes is the boundary of the rectangles in
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{W})}
, it is necessary that, apart from the boundary periodic codes of
\mathcal{R}
, the boundary refinement also includes the projection of all the codes in
\mathcal{W}
and, consequently, the whole orbits of such points.

Finally, Corollary
17
establishes the existence of such an algorithm. Thus, we have completed the proof of our theorem.
∎

6.5.
The
u
-boundary refinement.

In this part, we are going to define the
u
-boundary refinement of a Markov partition along a family of periodic codes. This procedure involves cutting
{\mathcal{R}}
along the unstable segments that correspond to the orbit of any of the codes in
{\mathcal{W}}
. In fact, we can define it using our previous
s
-boundary refinement applied to
T^{-1}
and
f^{-1}
.

Definition 68
.

Let
T
be a geometric type in the pseudo-Anosov class with an incidence matrix
A:=A(T)
that is binary. Consider a generalized pseudo-Anosov homeomorphism
f:S\rightarrow S
with a geometric Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}
of geometric type
T
.
Let

{\mathcal{W}}=\{\underline{w}^{1},\cdots,\underline{w}^{Q}\},

be a family of periodic codes that are non-
u
-boundary.

•

Let
{\mathcal{R}}_{U({\mathcal{W}})}
the
s
-boundary refinement of
{\mathcal{R}}
when
{\mathcal{R}}
is viewed as a Markov partition of
f^{-1}
. i.e
({\mathcal{R}},f^{-1})
. Denote by
T^{-1}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
the geometric type of
({\mathcal{R}}_{U({\mathcal{W}})},f^{-1})
.

•

Let
T_{U({\mathcal{W}})}
the geometric type of
({\mathcal{R}}_{U({\mathcal{W}})},f)
.

In this case the geometry Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}_{U({\mathcal{W}})}
for
f
called the
u
-boundary refinement of
{\mathcal{R}}
respect the family
{\mathcal{W}}
.

Remark 3
.

We can make the following direct observations:

(1)

If
\underline{w}
is not a
u
-boundary point for
({\mathcal{R}},f)
, the
\underline{w}
it is not a
s
-boundary point for
({\mathcal{R}},f^{-1})
, which justifies our assumption.

(2)

The geometric Markov partition
({\mathcal{R}}_{U({\mathcal{W}})},f)
has a geometric type
T_{U({\mathcal{W}})}
, and this geometric type is the inverse of the geometric type of
({\mathcal{R}}_{U({\mathcal{W}})},f^{-1})
, therefore:

T_{U({\mathcal{W}})}:=(T^{-1}_{S({\mathcal{W}})})^{-1}.

and we can determine
T_{U({\mathcal{W}})}
in an algorithmic manner.

(3)

For every
\underline{w}\in{\mathcal{W}}
, the point
\pi_{f}(\underline{w})
(where
\pi_{f}
is the projection with respect to
({\mathcal{R}},f)
) is a
u
-boundary point of
{\mathcal{R}}_{U({\mathcal{W}})}
.

(4)

If
\underline{w}
is a
u
-boundary point, clearly
{\mathcal{R}}_{U(\underline{w})}={\mathcal{R}}
and
T_{U(\underline{w})}=T
. This justifies taking any code in
{\mathcal{W}}
as a non
u
-boundary code.

7.
The corner refinement

Given a couple of homomorphism/geometric Markov partition
(f,{\mathcal{R}})
and a family of periodic codes
{\mathcal{W}}
, we are interested in concatenating the
u
and
s
boundary refinements to produce a refinement of
(f,{\mathcal{R}})
, namely
(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{C}}})
, with the corner property and such that the only periodic codes are those on the boundary of
{\mathcal{R}}
. The following theorem describes this situation.

Theorem 8
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{T}(\textbf{p-A})^{SIM}
and let
(f,{\mathcal{R}})
be a pair that represents
T
. Then there exists a geometric Markov partition of
f
, denoted
{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{C}}}
, called the corner refinement of
(f,{\mathcal{R}})
with the following properties:

(1)

The geometric Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{C}}}
has the corner property.

(2)

The periodic boundary codes of
(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{C}}}
are the boundary periodic points of
(f,{\mathcal{R}})
.

(3)

There exists an algorithm and explicit formulas to compute the geometric type
T_{{\mathcal{C}}}
of
(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{C}}}
.

In the next sub-section, we will develop the theory to determine the boundary codes of every geometric type, and then proceed to construct and compute the geometric type of the corner refinement. This intermediate step is necessary in order to obtain an algoritmic description od the geometric
T_{{\mathcal{C}}}

7.1.
Boundary codes and
s,u
-generating functions

Let’s proceed with the construction of the codes that are projected onto the boundary of the Markov partition,
\partial^{s,u}{\mathcal{R}}
. We assume that
{\mathcal{R}}=\{R_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
is a geometric Markov partition of
f
with geometric type
T
. For each
i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
, we label the boundary components of
R_{i}
as follows:

•

\partial^{s}_{+1}R_{i}
denotes the upper stable boundary of the rectangle
R_{i}
.

•

\partial^{s}_{-1}R_{i}
denotes the lower stable boundary of
R_{i}
.

•

\partial^{u}_{-1}R_{i}
denotes the left unstable boundary of
R_{i}
.

•

\partial^{u}_{+1}R_{i}
denotes the right unstable boundary of
R_{i}
.

By using these labeling conventions, we can uniquely identify the boundary components of
{\mathcal{R}}
based on the geometric type
T
.

Definition 69
.

Let
T=\{n,\{(h_{i},v_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n},\Phi_{T}\}
be an abstract geometric type. The
s
-
boundary labels
of
T
are defined as the formal set:

{\mathcal{S}}(T):=\{(i,\epsilon):i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}\text{ and }\epsilon\in\{1,-1\}\},

Similarly, the
u
-
boundary labels
of
T
are defined as the formal set:

{\mathcal{U}}(T):=\{(k,\epsilon):k\in\{1,\cdots,n\}\text{ and }\epsilon\in\{1,-1\}\}

In a while, we will justify such names. It’s important to note that this definition was made using only the value of
n
given by the geometric type
T
, so it does not depend on the specific realization. With these labels, we can formulate in terms of the geometric type the codes that
\pi_{f}
projects to
\partial^{u,s}_{\pm 1}R_{i}
. The first step is to introduce a
generating function

We begin by relabeling the stable boundary component of
\mathcal{R}
using the next function:

\theta_{T}:\{1,\cdots,n\}\times\{1,-1\}\rightarrow\{1,\cdots,n\}\times\cup_{i=1}^{n}\{1,h_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset{\mathcal{H}}(T)

that is defined as:

(42)

\theta_{T}(i,-1)=1\text{ and }\theta(i,1)=h_{i}.

The effect of
\theta_{T}
is to choose the sub-rectangle of
R_{i}
that contains the stable boundary component in question. Specifically, the boundary
\partial^{s}_{-1}R_{1}
is contained in
\partial^{s}_{-1}H^{i}_{1}
, and the boundary
\partial^{s}_{+1}R_{1}
is contained in
\partial^{s}_{+1}H^{i}_{h_{i}}
. This allows us to track the image of a stable boundary component, as the image of
\partial^{s}_{+1}R_{i}
under
f
should be contained in the stable boundary of
f(H^{i}_{h_{i}})
. But remember, the image of
\partial^{s}_{-1}R_{i}
is a boundary component of
f(H^{i}_{1})=V^{k}_{l}
, and the pair
(k,l)
is uniquely determined by
\Phi_{T}
. By considering the value of
\epsilon_{T}(i,h_{i})
, we can trace the image of the upper boundary component of
H^{i}_{h_{i}}
in a more precise manner. If
\epsilon_{T}(i,h_{i})=1
, it indicates that the map
f
does not alter the vertical orientations. As a result, the image of the upper boundary component of
H^{i}_{h_{i}}
will remain on the upper boundary component of
f(H^{i}_{h_{i}})
. i.e. in this example:

f(\partial^{s}_{+1}R_{i})\subset f(\partial^{s}_{+1}H^{i}_{h_{i}})\subset\partial^{s}_{+1}R_{k}.

On the contrary, if
\epsilon_{T}(i,h_{i})=-1
, it means that the map
f
changes the vertical orientation. This has the following implication:

f(\partial^{s}_{+1}R_{i})\subset f(\partial^{s}_{+1}H^{i}_{h_{i}})\subset\partial_{-1}R_{k}.

We don’t really care about the index
l
in
(k,l)\in\mathcal{V}(T)
, so it’s convenient to decompose
\rho_{T}
into two parts:
\rho_{T}:=(\xi_{T},\nu_{T})
. In this decomposition

\xi_{T}:{\mathcal{H}}(T)\rightarrow\{1,\cdots,n\},

is defined as
\xi_{T}(i,j)=k
if and only if
\rho_{T}(i,j)=(k,l)
.

Let’s continue with our example. In order to determine where
f
sends the upper boundary component
\partial^{s}_{+1}R_{i}
, we need to identify the rectangle in
\mathcal{R}
to which
f
maps
H^{i}_{j}
. This can be determined using the following relation:

\xi_{T}(i,\theta_{T}(1))=\xi_{T}(i,h_{i})=k.

We now incorporate the change of orientation to determine the boundary component of
R_{k}
that contains
f(\partial^{s}_{+1}H^{i}_{h_{i}})
. If we assume that
f(\partial^{s}_{+1}R_{i})\subset\partial^{s}_{\epsilon}R_{k}
, then the value of
\epsilon
can be determined using the following formula:

\epsilon=+1\cdot\epsilon_{T}(i,h_{i})=+1\cdot\epsilon_{T}(i,\theta_{T}(i,1)).

This procedure is illustrated in Figure
16
.

Figure 16.
The effect of
\theta_{T}

The
S
-generating function utilizes
\theta_{T}
and
\xi_{T}
to generalize this idea.

Definition 70
.

The
s
-generating function of
T
is the function

\Gamma(T):{\mathcal{S}}(T)\rightarrow{\mathcal{S}}(T),

defined for every
s
-boundary label
(i_{0},\epsilon_{0})\in\mathcal{S}(T)
by the following formula:

(43)

\Gamma(T)(i_{0},\epsilon_{0})=(\xi_{T}(i_{0},\theta_{T}(i_{0},\epsilon_{0})),\epsilon_{0}\cdot\epsilon_{T}(i_{0},\theta_{T}(i_{0},\epsilon_{0}))).

The
u
-boundary generating function can be defined as either the
s
-boundary generating function of
T^{-1}
(associated with
f^{-1}
), or it can be defined directly as follows.

Definition 71
.

The
u
-
generating function
of
T
is

\Upsilon(T):{\mathcal{U}}(T)\rightarrow{\mathcal{U}}(T).

defined as the
s
-
generating function
of
T^{-1}
,
\Gamma(T^{-1})
.

The orbit of a label
(i_{0},\epsilon_{0})\in{\mathcal{S}}(T)
under
\Gamma(T)
is defined as:

\{(i_{m},\epsilon_{m})\}_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}:=\{\Gamma(T)^{m}(i_{0},\epsilon_{0}):m\in{\mathbb{N}}\}.

We define
\varsigma_{T}:{\mathcal{S}}(T)\rightarrow{1,\dots,n}
as the projection onto the first component of the funtion
\Gamma_{T}
, i.e
\varsigma_{T}(i,\epsilon)=k
if and only if
\Gamma_{T}(i,j)=(k,\epsilon)
. We will focus on the
s
-generating function. The ideas for the unstable case naturally extend, but sometimes we are going to recall them too.

The generating functions allow us to create a positive (or negative) code in
\Sigma^{+}
for every
s
-boundary label of
T
by composing
\Gamma(T)
(
\Upsilon(T)
) with
\varsigma_{T}
to obtain:

(44)

\displaystyle\underline{I}^{+}(i_{0},\epsilon_{0}):=\{\varsigma_{T}\circ\Gamma(T)^{m}(i_{0},\epsilon_{0})\}_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}=\{i_{m}\}_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}

Similarly, for
(k_{0},\epsilon_{0})\in{\mathcal{U}}(T)
, we can construct the negative code in
\Sigma^{-}
given by:

(45)

\displaystyle\underline{J}^{-}(k_{0},\epsilon_{0}):=\{\varsigma_{T}\circ\Upsilon(T)^{m}(k_{0},\epsilon_{0})\}_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}=\{k_{-m}\}_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}

These positive and negative codes are of great importance and deserve a name.

Definition 72
.

Let
(i_{0},\epsilon_{0})\in{\mathcal{S}}(T)
. The
s
-
boundary positive code
of
(i_{0},\epsilon_{0})
is denoted as
\underline{I}^{+}(i_{0},\epsilon_{0})\in\Sigma^{+}
, and it is determined by Equation
44
. The set of
s
-boundary positive codes of
T
is denoted as:

\underline{{\mathcal{S}}}^{+}(T)=\{\underline{I}^{+}(i,\epsilon):(i,\epsilon)\in{\mathcal{S}}(T)\}\subset\Sigma^{+}.

Let
(k_{0},\epsilon_{0})\in{\mathcal{U}}(T)
. The
u
-
boundary negative code
of
(k_{0},\epsilon_{0})
is denoted as
\underline{J}^{-}(k_{0},\epsilon_{0})=\{k_{-m}\}_{m=0}^{\infty}
and is determined by Equation
45
. The set of
u
-boundary negative codes of
T
is denoted as

\underline{{\mathcal{J}}}^{-}(T)=\{\underline{J}^{-}(k,\epsilon):(k,\epsilon)\in{\mathcal{U}}(T)\}\subset\Sigma^{-}

Lemma
49
below asserts that every
s
-boundary positive code of
T
corresponds to the positive part of at least one element of
\Sigma_{A}
. The analogous result for
u
-boundary negative codes is true using a fully symmetric approach with the
u
-generating function. Therefore, we will only provide the proof for the stable case.

Lemma 49
.

Every
s
-boundary positive code
\underline{I}^{+}(i_{0},\epsilon_{0})
belongs to
\Sigma_{A}^{+}
. Similarly, every
u
-boundary negative code
\underline{J}^{-}(k_{0},\epsilon_{0})
belongs to
\Sigma_{A}^{-}
.

Proof.

The incidence matrix
A
of
T
has a relation to any realization
(f,{\mathcal{R}})
of
T
, which we can exploit to visualize the situation, anyway observe that our arguments only use the properties of
A
or
T
.
In the construction of the positive code
\underline{I}^{+}(i_{0},\epsilon_{0})
, the term
i_{1}
corresponds to the index of the unique rectangle in
{\mathcal{R}}
such that
f(H^{i_{0}}_{\theta_{T}(1,\epsilon_{0})})=V^{i_{1}}_{l_{1}}
(where
\theta_{T}(i_{0},\epsilon)=1
or
h_{i}
). In either case, we have:

f^{-1}(\overset{o}{R{i_{1}}})\cap\overset{o}{R_{i_{0}}}=\overset{o}{H^{i_{0}}_{\theta_{T}(1,\epsilon_{0})}}\neq\emptyset,

which implies
a_{i_{0},i_{1}}=1
. By induction, we can indeed extend this result to the entire positive code
\underline{I}^{+}(i_{0},\epsilon_{0})
.

Similarly, for the case of
u
-boundary negative codes, we can use the symmetric approach by considering
T^{-1}
and the inverse of the incidence matrix
A^{-1}
. Additionally, we can consider the realization
(f^{-1},{\mathcal{R}})
to obtain a visualization.
∎

Proposition
12
is the first step in proving that each
s
-boundary label uniquely determines a set of codes that projects to a single boundary component of the partition.

Proposition 12
.

The map
I:{\mathcal{S}}(T)\rightarrow\Sigma_{A}^{+}
defined by
I(i,\epsilon):=\underline{I}^{+}(i,\epsilon)
is injective map. Similarly, the map
J:{\mathcal{U}}(T)\rightarrow\Sigma_{A}^{-}
defined by
J(i,\epsilon):=\underline{J}^{-}(i,\epsilon)
is also injective.

Proof.

Clearly
I
is a well defined map as
\Gamma^{n}(T)
is itself a well defined map with the same domain. If
i_{0}\neq i^{\prime}_{0}
, then the sequences
\underline{I}^{+}(i_{0},\epsilon_{0})
and
\underline{I}^{+}(i^{\prime}_{0},\epsilon^{\prime}_{0})
will differ in their first term, making them distinct. The remaining case involves considering
(i_{0},1)
and
(i_{0},-1)
.

Let’s denote
\underline{I}^{+}(i_{0},1)={i_{m}}
and
\underline{I}^{+}(i_{0},-1)={i^{\prime}_{m}}
as their positive codes. We will analyze the sequences
{\Gamma(T)^{m}(i_{0},1)}
and
{\Gamma(T)^{m}(i_{0},-1)}
and show that there exists an
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
i_{m}\neq i^{\prime}_{m}
. Our approach starts with the following technical lemma:

Lemma 50
.

If
T
is in the pseudo-Anosov class and
A
is a binary matrix, then there exists
M\in\mathbb{N}
such that if
\underline{I}^{+}(i_{0},1)=\{i_{m}\}_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, then
h_{i_{M}}>1
.

Proof.

Since
T
is in the pseudo-Anosov class, there exists a realization
(f,\mathcal{R})
. The infinite sequence
{i_{m}}
takes a finite number of values (at most
n
), so there exist natural numbers
m_{1}<m_{2}
such that
R_{i_{m_{1}}}=R_{i_{m_{2}}}
. If such an
M
does not exist, then for all
m_{1}\leq m\leq m_{2}
,
h_{i_{m}}=1
. This implies that
f^{m_{2}-m_{1}}(R_{i_{m_{1}}})\subset R_{i_{m_{2}}}=R_{i_{m_{1}}}
, which is a vertical sub-rectangle of
R_{i_{m_{1}}}
.

By uniform expansion in the vertical direction, the rectangle
R_{i_{m_{1}}}
reduces to a stable interval, and this is not permitted in a Markov partition, leading us to a contradiction. ∎

In view of Lemma
M:=\min\{m\in{\mathbb{N}}:h_{i_{m}}>1\}
exists. If there is
0\leq m\leq M
such that
i_{m}\neq i^{\prime}_{m}
, our proof is complete. If not, the following Lemma addresses the remaining situation.

Lemma 51
.

Suppose that for all
0\leq m\leq M
,
i_{m}=i_{m}^{\prime}
, then
i_{M+1}\neq i^{\prime}_{M+1}
.

Proof.

Observe that for all
0\leq m\leq M
, if
\Gamma(T)^{m}(i_{0},+1)=(i_{m},\epsilon_{m})
then

\Gamma(T)^{m}(i_{0},-1)=(i_{m},-\epsilon_{m})

.

They have the same index
i_{m}=i^{\prime}_{m}
but still have inverse
\epsilon
part, i.e.,
\epsilon_{m}=-\epsilon^{\prime}_{m}
. In fact, the first term
\epsilon_{0}=-\epsilon^{\prime}_{0}
, without lost of generality
\epsilon_{0}=1
and
\epsilon^{\prime}_{0}=-1
, by hypothesis and considering that
1=h_{i_{0}}=h_{i^{\prime}_{0}}
we infer that:

\theta_{T}(i_{0},\epsilon_{0})=(i_{0},h_{i_{m}})=(i^{\prime}_{0},1)=\theta_{T}(i^{\prime}_{0},\epsilon_{0}^{\prime}),

Therefore:

\epsilon_{1}=\epsilon_{0}\cdot\epsilon_{T}(i_{0},h_{i_{0}})=-\epsilon^{\prime}_{0}\epsilon_{T}(i^{\prime}_{0},1)=-\epsilon^{\prime}_{1}.

then continue the argument by induction. In particular:
\Gamma(T)^{M}(i_{0},1)=(i_{M},\epsilon_{M})
and
\Gamma(T)^{M}(i^{\prime}_{0},-1)=(i_{M},-\epsilon_{M})
.

The incidence matrix of
T
have
\{0,1\}
has coefficients
{0,1}
, and since that
1\neq h_{i_{M}}
, if
\rho_{T}(i_{M},1)=(k,l)
then
\rho_{T}(i_{M},h_{i_{M}})=(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
where
k\neq k^{\prime}
.

Consider the case when,
\theta_{T}(i_{M},\epsilon_{M})=(i_{M},1)
and
\theta_{T}(i_{M},\epsilon^{\prime}_{M})=(i_{M},h_{i_{M}})
. Lets apply the formula of
\Gamma(T)
:

\Gamma(T)^{M+1}(i_{0},1)=\Gamma(T)(i_{M},\epsilon_{M})=(\xi_{T}(i_{M},1),\epsilon_{M}\cdot\epsilon_{T}(i_{M},1))=(k,\epsilon_{M+1})

and

\Gamma(T)^{M+1}(i_{0},-1)=\Gamma(T)(i_{M},-\epsilon_{M})=(\xi_{T}(i_{M},h_{i_{M}}),-\epsilon_{M}\cdot\epsilon_{T}(i_{M},h_{i_{M}}))=(k^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime}_{M+1})

therefore
i_{M+1}=k\neq k^{\prime}=i^{\prime}_{M+1}
.

The situation
\theta_{T}(i_{M},\epsilon_{M})=(i_{M},h_{i_{M}})
and
\theta_{T}(i_{M},\epsilon^{\prime}_{M})=(i_{M},1)
is treated similarly. The has been lemma proved.
∎

The proposition follows from the previous lemma. The result for negative codes associated with
u
-boundary labels is proven using a fully symmetric approach with the
u
-generating function.
∎

If
\Gamma(T)(i,\epsilon)=(i_{1},\epsilon_{1})
, applying the shift to this code gives another
s
-boundary positive code. That is clearly given by

\sigma(\underline{I}^{+}(i,\epsilon))=\underline{I}^{+}(i_{1},\epsilon_{1}),

where
\Gamma(T)(i_{0},\epsilon_{0})=(i_{1},\epsilon_{1})
. Since there are
2n
different
s
-boundary positive codes, there exist natural numbers
k_{1}\neq k_{2}
with
k_{1},k_{2}\leq 2n
such that
\sigma^{k_{1}}(\underline{I}^{+}(i,\epsilon))=\sigma^{k_{2}}(\underline{I}^{+}(i,\epsilon))
, and the code
\underline{I}^{+}(i,\epsilon)
is pre-periodic. This implies the following corollary.

Corollary 18
.

There are exactly
2n
different
s
-boundary positive codes and
2n
different
u
-boundary negative codes. Furthermore, every
s
-boundary positive code and every
u
-boundary negative code is pre-periodic under the action of the shift
\sigma
.

Moreover, for every
s
-boundary positive code
\underline{I}^{+}(i,\epsilon)
, there exists
k\leq 2n
such that
\sigma^{k}(\underline{I}^{+}(i,\epsilon))
is periodic. Similarly, for every
u
-boundary negative code
\underline{J}^{-}(i,\epsilon)
, there exists
k\leq 2n
such that
\sigma^{-k}(\underline{J}^{-}(i,\epsilon))
is periodic.

Now we are ready to define a family of admissible codes that project onto the stable and unstable leaves of periodic boundary points of
{\mathcal{R}}
.

Definition 73
.

The set of
s
-
boundary codes
of
T
is:

(46)

\underline{{\mathcal{S}}}(T):=\{\underline{w}\in\Sigma_{A}:\underline{w}_{+}\in\underline{{\mathcal{S}}}^{+}(T)\}.

The set of
u
-
boundary codes
of
T
is

(47)

\underline{{\mathcal{U}}}(T):=\{\underline{w}\in\Sigma_{A}:\underline{w}_{-}\in\underline{{\mathcal{U}}(T)}^{-}(T)\}.

Next, Proposition
13
states that the projection of
s
-boundary codes of
T
through
\pi^{f}
is always contained in the stable boundary of the Markov partition
(f,{\mathcal{R}})
. Similarly, Proposition
14
ensures that these are the only codes in
\Sigma_{A}
that project to the stable boundary. This provides us with a symbolic characterization of the boundary of the Markov partition. As we have done so far, we will provide a detailed proof for the stable case, noting that the unstable version follows by symmetry.

Proposition 13
.

Let
(f,{\mathcal{R}})
be a pair consisting of a homeomorphism and a partition that realizes
T
. Suppose
(i,\epsilon)\in{\mathcal{S}}(T)
is an
s
-boundary label of
T
, and let
\underline{w}\in\underline{{\mathcal{S}}}(T)
be a code such that
\underline{I}^{+}(i,\epsilon)=\underline{w}_{+}
. Then,
\pi_{f}(\underline{w})\in\partial^{s}_{\epsilon}R_{i}
.

Similarly, if
(i,\epsilon)\in{\mathcal{U}}(T)
is a
u
-boundary label of
T
, and
\underline{w}\in\underline{{\mathcal{U}}}(T)
is a code such that
\underline{J}^{-}(i,\epsilon)=\underline{w}_{-}
, then
\pi_{f}(\underline{w})\in\partial^{u}_{\epsilon}R_{i}
.

Proof.

Make
(i,\epsilon)=(i_{0},\epsilon_{0})
to make coherent the following notation and
\underline{I}^{+}(i,\epsilon)=\{i_{m}\}_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
. For every
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
define the rectangles
\overset{o}{H_{s}}=\cap_{m=0}^{s}f^{-1}(\overset{o}{R_{i_{m}}})
. The limit of the closures of such rectangles when
s
converge to infinity is a unique stable segment of
R_{i_{0}}
. The proof is achieved if
\partial^{s}_{\epsilon_{0}}R_{i_{0}}\subset H_{s}:=\overline{\overset{o}{H_{s}}}
for all
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
. In this order of ideas is enough to argument that for all
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
:

f^{s}(\partial^{s}_{\epsilon_{0}}R_{i_{0}})\subset R_{i_{s}}.

We are going to probe by induction over
s
something more specific:

f^{s}(\partial_{\epsilon_{0}}R_{i_{0}})\subset\partial_{\epsilon_{s}}R_{i_{s}}.

Base of induction
: For
s=0
. This is the case as
f^{0}(\partial^{s}_{\epsilon_{0}}R_{i_{0}})\subset\partial^{s}_{\epsilon_{0}}R_{i_{0}}
.

Hypothesis of induction
: Assume that
f^{s}(\partial_{\epsilon_{0}}R_{i_{0}})\subset\partial_{\epsilon_{s}}R_{i_{s}}
and
f^{s}(\partial^{s}_{\epsilon_{0}}R_{i_{0}})\subset R_{i_{s}}
.

Induction step
: We are going to prove that

f^{s+1}(\partial_{\epsilon_{0}}R_{i_{0}})\subset\partial_{\epsilon_{s+1}}R_{i_{s+1}}

and then that
f^{s+1}(\partial^{s}_{\epsilon_{0}}R_{i_{0}})\subset R_{i_{s+1}}
. For that reason consider the two following cases:

•

\epsilon_{s}=1
. In this situation
f^{s}(\partial^{s}_{\epsilon_{0}}R_{i_{0}})\subset H^{i_{s}}_{h_{i_{s}}}
. Hence
f^{s+1}(\partial^{s}_{i_{0}}R_{i_{0}})\subset f(H^{i_{s}}_{h_{i_{s}}})\subset R_{i^{\prime}_{s+1}}
. Where
R_{i^{\prime}_{s+1}}
is the only rectangle such that
\xi_{T}(i_{s},h_{i_{s}})=(i^{\prime}_{s+1})
. Even more
f^{s+1}(\partial^{s}_{\epsilon_{0}}R_{i_{0}})\subset\partial^{s}_{\epsilon^{\prime}_{s+1}}R_{i^{\prime}_{s+1}}
, where
\epsilon^{\prime}_{s+1}
obey to the formula
\epsilon^{\prime}_{s+1}=\epsilon_{T}(i_{s},h_{i_{s}})=\epsilon_{s}\cdot\epsilon_{T}(i_{s},h_{i_{s}})
.

•

\epsilon_{s}=-1
. In this situation
f^{s}(\partial^{s}_{\epsilon_{0}}R_{i_{0}})\subset H^{i_{s}}_{1}
. Hence
f^{s+1}(\partial^{s}_{i_{0}}R_{i_{0}})\subset f(H^{i_{s}}_{1})\subset R_{i^{\prime}_{s+1}}
, where
R_{i_{s+1}}
is the only rectangle such that
\xi_{T}(i_{s},1)=(i^{\prime}_{s+1}
.
Even more,
f^{s+1}(\partial^{s}_{\epsilon_{0}}R_{i_{0}})\subset\partial^{s}_{\epsilon^{\prime}_{s+1}}R_{i^{\prime}_{s+1}}
, where
\epsilon^{\prime}_{s+1}
obey to the formula
\epsilon^{\prime}_{s+1}=-\epsilon_{T}(i_{s},1)=\epsilon_{s}\cdot\epsilon_{T}(i_{s},1)
.

In bot situations:

f^{s+1}(\partial^{s}_{\epsilon_{0}})R_{i_{0}})\subset\partial^{s}_{\epsilon^{\prime}_{s+1}}R_{i^{\prime}_{s+1}}

and they follows the rule:

(i^{\prime}_{s+1},\epsilon^{\prime}_{s+1})=(\xi_{T}(i_{s},\theta_{T}(i_{s},\epsilon_{s})),\epsilon_{s}\cdot\epsilon_{T}(i_{s},\theta_{T}(\epsilon_{s})))=\Gamma(T)^{s+1}(i_{0},\epsilon_{0})=(i_{s+1},\epsilon_{s+1}).

Therefore
f^{s+1}(\partial_{\epsilon_{0}}R_{i_{0}})\subset\partial_{\epsilon_{s+1}}R_{i_{s+1}}
, as we claimed and the result is proved.
The unstable case is totally symmetric.

∎

Proposition
13
provides justification for naming the
s
-boundary and
u
-boundary labels of
T
as such, as they generate codes that are projected to the boundary of the Markov partition. The next proposition asserts that these codes are the only ones that have such a property.

Proposition 14
.

If
\underline{w}\in\Sigma_{A}
projects to the stable boundary of the Markov partition
(f,{\mathcal{R}})
under
\pi_{f}
, i.e.,
\pi_{f}(\underline{w})\in\partial^{s}{\mathcal{R}}
, then it follows that
\underline{w}\in\underline{{\mathcal{S}}}(T)
. Similarly, if
\pi_{f}(\underline{w})\in\partial^{u}{\mathcal{R}}
, then
\underline{w}\in\underline{{\mathcal{U}}}(T)
.

Proof.

Like
A(T)
has coefficients
{0,1}
, the sequence
\underline{w}_{+}
determines, for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
, a pair
(w_{m},j_{m})\in\mathcal{H}(T)
such that
\xi_{T}(w_{m},j_{m})=w_{m+1}
and a number
\epsilon_{T}(w_{m},j_{m})=\epsilon_{m+1}\in{1,-1}
. It is important to note that, like
f^{m}(x)\in\partial^{s}{\mathcal{R}}
, in fact, there are only two cases (unless
h_{w_{m}}=1
where they are the same):

w_{m+1}=\xi_{T}(w_{m},1)\text{ or well }w_{m+1}=\xi_{T}(w_{m},h_{w_{m}}).

This permit to define
\epsilon_{m}\in\{-1,+1\}
as the only number such that:

(48)

w_{m+1}=\xi_{T}(w_{m},\theta_{T}(w_{m},\epsilon_{m})).

Even more
\epsilon_{m}
determine
\epsilon_{m+1}
by the formula:

\epsilon_{m+1}=\epsilon_{m}\cdot\epsilon_{T}(w_{m},\theta_{T}(w_{m},\epsilon_{m})).

In resume:

(49)

\Gamma(T)(w_{m},\epsilon_{m})=(w_{m+1},\epsilon_{m+1})

follow the rule dictated by the
s
-generating function. So if we know
\epsilon_{M}
for certain
M\in{\mathbb{N}}
we can determine
\sigma^{M}(\underline{w})_{+}=\underline{I}^{+}(w_{M},\epsilon_{M})
, so it rest to determine at least one
\epsilon_{M}
.

Lemma
50
can be adapted to this context to prove the existence of a minimal
M\in\mathbb{N}
such that
h_{w_{M}}>1
. Then, equation
48
determines
\epsilon_{M}
. Now, we need to recover
\epsilon_{0}
, but we proceed backwards. Since
h_{w_{m}}=1
for all
m<1
, we have:

\epsilon_{M}=\epsilon_{M-1}\cdot\epsilon_{T}(w_{M-1},1)

and then
\epsilon_{M-1}=\epsilon_{M}\cdot\epsilon_{T}(w_{M-1},1)
. By applied this procedure we can determine
\epsilon_{0}
and then using
49
to get that

\Gamma(T)^{m}(w_{0},\epsilon_{0})=(w_{m},\epsilon_{m}).

The conclusion is that
\underline{w}_{+}=\underline{I}^{+}(w_{0},\epsilon_{0})
.

The unstable boundary situation is analogous.
∎

Therefore,
\underline{{\mathcal{S}}}(T)
and
\underline{{\mathcal{U}}}(T)
are the only admissible codes that project to the boundary of a Markov partition. With this in mind, we can distinguish the periodic boundary codes from the non-periodic ones. It is important to note that the cardinality of each of these sets is less than or equal to
2n
.

Definition 74
.

The set of
s
-
boundary periodic codes
of
T
is:

(50)

\text{ Per }(\underline{{\mathcal{S}}(T)}):=\{\underline{w}\in\underline{{\mathcal{S}}}(T):\underline{w}\text{ is periodic }\}.

The set of
u
-
boundary periodic codes
of
T
is

(51)

\text{ Per }(\underline{{\mathcal{U}}(T)}):=\{\underline{w}\in\underline{{\mathcal{U}}}(T):\underline{w}\text{ is periodic }\}.

7.1.1.
Decomposition of
\Sigma_{A}

Now we can describe the stable and unstable leaves of
f
corresponding to the periodic points of the boundary.

Definition 75
.

We define the
s
-
boundary leaves codes
of
T
:

(52)

\Sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}(T)}=\{\underline{w}\in\Sigma_{A}:\exists k\in{\mathbb{N}}\text{ such that }\sigma^{k}(\underline{w})\in\underline{{\mathcal{S}}(T)}\}.

We define the
u
-
boundary leaves codes
of
T

(53)

\Sigma_{{\mathcal{U}}(T)}=\{\underline{w}\in\Sigma_{A}:\exists k\in{\mathbb{N}}\text{ such that }\sigma^{-k}(\underline{w})\in\underline{{\mathcal{U}}(T)}\}.

Finally, the
totally interior codes
of
T
are

\Sigma_{{\mathcal{I}}nt(T)}=\Sigma_{A}\setminus(\Sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}(T)}\cup\Sigma_{{\mathcal{U}}(T)}).

The importance of such a division of
\Sigma_{A}
is that its projections are well determined, as indicated by the following lemma.

Lemma 52
.

A code
\underline{w}\in\Sigma_{A}
belongs to
\Sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}(T)}
if and only if its projection
\pi_{f}(\underline{w})
is within the stable leaf of a boundary periodic point of
{\mathcal{R}}
.

A code
\underline{w}\in\Sigma_{A}
belongs to
\Sigma_{U(T)}
if and only if its projection
\pi_{f}(\underline{w})
lies within the unstable leaf of a boundary periodic point of
{\mathcal{R}}
.

A code
\underline{w}\in\Sigma_{A}
belongs to
\Sigma_{{\mathcal{I}}nt(T)}
if and only if its projection
\pi_{f}(\underline{w}
is contained within Int
(f,{\mathcal{R}})
¿

Proof.

The
s
-boundary leaf codes satisfy Definition
46
, and according to Proposition
11
, if
\underline{w}\in\Sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}(T)}
, their projection lies on the same stable manifold as an
s
-boundary component of
{\mathcal{R}}
. Stable boundary components of a Markov partition represent the stable manifold of an
s
-boundary periodic point, and for each
\underline{w}\in\Sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}(T)}
, there exists
k=k(\underline{w})\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\sigma^{k}(\underline{w})_{+}
is a periodic positive code (Corollary
18
). This positive code corresponds to a periodic point on the boundary of
{\mathcal{R}}
within whose stable manifold
\pi_{f}(\underline{w})
is contained. This proves one direction in the first assertion of the lemma.

If
\underline{v}
is a periodic boundary code ,
\pi_{f}(\underline{v})\in\partial^{s}{\mathcal{R}}
, by definition
\underline{v}\in\underline{{\mathcal{S}}(T)}
. Suppose that
\pi_{f}(\underline{w})
is on the stable leaf of
\underline{v}
, then there exist
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\pi_{f}(\sigma^{k}(\underline{w}))
is on the same stable boundary of
{\mathcal{R}}
as
\pi_{f}(\underline{v})
. The proposition
14
implies that
\sigma^{k}(\underline{w})\in\underline{{\mathcal{S}}(T)}
, hence
\underline{w}\in\Sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}(T)}
by definition. This completes the proof of the first assertion of the lemma. A similar argument proves the unstable case.

The conclusion of these items is that
\underline{w}\in\Sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}(T)}\cap\Sigma_{{\mathcal{U}}(T)}
if and only if
\pi_{f}(\underline{w})\in{\mathcal{F}}^{s,u}(\text{ Per }^{s,u}({\mathcal{R}}))
.

As proved in the Lemma
17
totally interior points are disjoint from the stable and unstable lamination generated by boundary periodic points. If
\underline{w}\in\Sigma_{{\mathcal{I}}nt(T)}
and
\pi_{f}(\underline{w})
is on the stable or unstable leaf of a
s,u
-boundary periodic point, we have seen that
\underline{w}\in\Sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}(T),{\mathcal{U}}(T)}
which is not possible, therefore
\pi_{f}(\underline{w})\in
Int
(f,{\mathcal{R}})
. In the conversely direction, if
\pi_{f}(\underline{w})
is not in the stable or unstable lamination of
s,u
-boundary points,
\underline{w}\notin(\Sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}(T)})\cup\Sigma_{{\mathcal{U}}(T)}
, so
\underline{w}\in\Sigma_{{\mathcal{I}}nt(T)}
. This ends the proof.
∎

We have obtained the decomposition

\Sigma_{A(T)}=\Sigma_{{\mathcal{I}}nt(T)}\cup\Sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}(T)}\cup\Sigma_{{\mathcal{U}}(T)}

and have characterized the image under
\pi_{f}
of each of these sets. In the next subsection we use this decomposition to define relations on each of these parts and then extend them to an equivalence relation in
\Sigma_{A}
.

7.2.
The corner refinement

Let
T
be a geometric type within the pseudo-Anosov class with binary incidence matrix
A(T)
. Consider a generalized pseudo-Anosov homeomorphism
f:S\to S
with a geometric Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}
of geometric type
T
. The projection
\pi_{(f,{\mathcal{R}})}:\Sigma_{A(T)}\to S
, as defined in
43
, depends on the specific geometric type
T
of the Markov partition. We will relabel it as
\pi_{T}:\Sigma_{A(T)}\to S
to enable easier comparison with another projection
\pi_{T^{\prime}}
associated with a different geometric type
T^{\prime}

For a given geometric type
T
, we have defined its
s
and
u
generating functions in
70
and
71
, respectively. Iterating these functions generates codes in
\Sigma_{A(T)}
, which project to the stable and unstable boundary components of
{\mathcal{R}}
. Furthermore, as described in sub-section
7.1
, using
T
, it is feasible to determine the sets of periodic
s
-boundary codes of
T
:

\textit{Per}(\underline{S(T)}):=\{\underline{w}\in\Sigma_{A(T)}:\underline{w}\text{ is periodic for $\sigma$ and }\pi_{T}(\underline{w})\in\partial^{s}{\mathcal{R}}\}.

and the sets of periodic
u
-boundary codes:

\textit{Per}(\underline{(T)}):=\{\underline{w}\in\Sigma_{A(T)}:\underline{w}\text{ is periodic for $\sigma$ and }\pi_{T}(\underline{w})\in\partial^{u}{\mathcal{R}}\}.

Their union is the set of boundary periodic codes:

\textit{Per}(\underline{B(T)}):=\textit{Per}(S(T))\cup\textit{Per}(U(T)).

In order to avoid to heavy notations we relabel these sets:

\underline{S(T)}:=\textit{Per}(\underline{S(T)}),\,\,\underline{U(T)}:=\textit{Per}(\underline{U(T)})\text{ and }\underline{B(T)}:=\textit{Per}(\underline{B(T)})

Finally a corner periodic code is any code in the intersection:

\underline{C(T)}:=\underline{S(T)}\cap\underline{U(T)}.

Definition
74
reveals that
\underline{B(T)}
can be described in a combinatorial manner using
T
, avoiding the use of the Markov partition or the homeomorphism
f
. Based on the theory developed in Section
7.1
, we can draw the following conclusion, which we present in the form of a corollary:

Corollary 19
.

Given a specific geometric type
T
, we can compute the sets:
\underline{(S(T)})
,
\underline{U(T)}
,
\underline{B(T)}
, and
\underline{C(T)}
, in terms of the geometric type
T
.

Definition 76
.

A geometric type
T
have the
corner property
if every periodic boundary code is a corner peridic code, i.e
\underline{B(T)}=\underline{S(T)}\cap\underline{U(T)}
.

We fix the following notation:

•

P_{s}(f)
is the set of
s
-boundary periodic points of
(f,{\mathcal{R}})
.

•

P_{u}(f)
is the set of
u
-boundary periodic points of
(f,{\mathcal{R}})

•

P_{u}(f)=P_{s}(f)\cup P_{u}(f)
is the set of periodic boundary codes of
(f,{\mathcal{R}})
, and

•

P_{c}(f)=P_{s}(f)\cap P_{u}(f)
is the set of corner points of
{\mathcal{R}}
.

Definition 77
.

A geometric Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}
of
f
have the
corner property
if every rectangle
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
that contains a boundary point
p\in P_{b}(f,{\mathcal{R}})
have
p
as a corner point.

A corner point
p
of a rectangle
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
is on the boundary of another rectangle
R^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{R}}
but it is not necessarily a corner point of
R^{\prime}
; it can be on the interior of the unstable boundary of
R^{\prime}
, for example. In this situation,
\pi_{T}^{-1}(p)\subset\Sigma_{A(T)}
contains a corner code and a
u
-boundary code that is not an
s
-boundary code. Therefore,
\underline{C(T)}\neq\underline{B(T)}
and
T
don’t have the corner property. This discrepancy makes it necessary to introduce the following lemma

Lemma 53
.

The geometric Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}
has the corner property if and only if its geometric type
T
has the corner property.

Proof.

If
{\mathcal{R}}
satisfies the corner property, and we have an
s
-boundary code
\underline{w}\in\underline{S(T)}
, then by Lemma
52
,
\pi_{T}(\underline{w})
is a stable boundary periodic point contained in the rectangle
R_{w_{0}}
, where
w_{0}=\underline{w}_{0}
, is
0
therm of the code
\underline{w}
. However, by hypothesis, if
p
is a
s
-boundary point of
R_{w_{0}}
, it is a corner point of
R_{w_{0}}
, then,
p
is a
u
-boundary point. We can use Lemma
52
to deduce that the code
\underline{w}
is also a
u
-boundary code. So,
\underline{w}\in\underline{C(T)}
. The case when the code
\underline{w}
is a
u
-boundary code, i.e.,
\underline{w}\in\underline{U(T)}
, is similarly proven.

On the other hand, assume
T
satisfies the corner property. Let
p\in R_{w_{0}}\in{\mathcal{R}}
a
s
-boundary point of
R
. There exist a
s
-boundary code
\underline{w}\in\pi^{-1}_{T}(p)
such that
\underline{w}_{0}=w_{0}
. Like
T
have the corner property, any
s
-boundary periodic code is
u
-boundary, which in turn means
\underline{w}
is a
u
-boundary code. As a consequence of Lemma
52
,
p=\pi_{T}(\underline{w})\in R_{w_{0}}
is a
u
-boundary point contained in
R_{w_{0}}
, therefore
p
is a corner point of such rectangle,
R_{w_{0}}
. The situation when
p
is
s
-boundary point of
R_{w_{0}}
is proved in the same manner.

On the other hand, assume that
T
satisfies the corner property. Let
p\in\in\partial^{s}R_{w_{0}}
be an
s
-boundary point of
R_{w_{0}}
. There exists an
s
-boundary code
\underline{w}\in\pi^{-1}_{T}(p)
such that
\underline{w}_{0}=w_{0}
. Since
T
has the corner property, any
s
-boundary periodic code is also a
u
-boundary code, which, in turn, means that
\underline{w}
is a
u
-boundary code. As a consequence of
52
,
p=\pi_{T}(\underline{w})\in\partial^{s}R{w_{0}}
is a
u
-boundary point contained in
R{w_{0}}
; therefore,
p
is a corner point of such a rectangle,
R_{w_{0}}
. The situation when
p
is an
s
-boundary point of
R_{w_{0}}
is proved in the same manner.

∎

7.2.1.
The construction of the corner refinement

The first goal of this section is to provide a method for creating a geometric Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{C}}}
for the homeomorphism
f
, along with its corresponding geometric type
T_{{\mathcal{C}}}
, in such a way that both
{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{C}}}
and
T_{{\mathcal{C}}}
satisfy the corner property.

Let
T
be a geometric type in the pseudo-Anosov class with a binary incidence matrix
A(T)
. Consider a generalized pseudo-Anosov homeomorphism
f:S\to S
with a geometric Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}
of geometric type
T
. The procedure is as describe in the following paragraph:

(1)

Get the
s
-boundary refinement of
{\mathcal{R}}
along the family of boundary periodic of codes
\underline{B(T)}
to obtain:

{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{S}}(\underline{B(T)})}.

and its geometric type:
T_{{\mathcal{S}}(\underline{B(T)})}
.

(2)

Compute the set of boundary periodic codes of the geometric type
T_{{\mathcal{S}}(\underline{B(T)})}
(Corollary
19
):

\underline{S(T_{{\mathcal{S}}(\underline{{\mathcal{B}}(T)})})}\subset\Sigma_{A(T_{{\mathcal{S}}(\underline{B(T)})})}.

(3)

Obtain the
u
-boundary refinement of
{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{S}}(\underline{B(T)})}
along the family of boundary periodic codes
\underline{S(T_{{\mathcal{S}}(\underline{B(T)})})}
to obtain the corner refinement of
{\mathcal{R}}
:

(54)

{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{C}}}:=[{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{S}}(\underline{B(T)})}]_{{\mathcal{U}}(\underline{S(T_{S(\underline{B(T)})})})}.

whose geometric type is
T_{{\mathcal{C}}}
.

Definition 78
.

The geometric Markov partition for
f
,
{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{C}}}
, described by equation (
54
), is the
Corner refinement
of the geometric Markov partition
(f,{\mathcal{R}})
, and its geometric type
T_{{\mathcal{C}}}
is the
Corner refinement
of
T
.

Proposition
15
implies Theorem
8
:

Proposition 15
.

Let
T\in{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{T}}(\textbf{p-A})^{SIM}
. Consider a
p-A
homeomorphism
f:S\to S
with a geometric Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}
of geometric type
T
. Let
(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{C}}})
be the corner refinement of
(f,{\mathcal{R}})
, and let
T_{{\mathcal{C}}}
be the geometric type of
(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{C}}})
. Then:

i)

The boundary periodic points of
(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{C}}})
and the boundary periodic points of
(f,{\mathcal{R}})
are exactly the same. In simpler terms:
P_{b}(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{C}}}=P_{b}(f,{\mathcal{R}})
.

ii)

The Markov partition
(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{C}}})
exhibits the corner property.

iii)

There exists an algorithm to compute the geometric type
T_{{\mathcal{C}}}
, which consists of computing the geometric type
T_{{\mathcal{S}}(B(T))}
of the
s
-boundary refinement of
(f,{\mathcal{R}})
along the family of periodic boundary codes of
{\mathcal{R}}
, and then computing the geometric type of the
u
-boundary refinement of
(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{S}}(\underline{B(T)})})
along the family of periodic boundary codes of
{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{S}}(\underline{B(T)})}
.

Proof.

Item

i)
. For the
s
-boundary refinement, the following equality holds:

P_{b}(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{S(\underline{B(T)}))}):=P_{b}(f,{\mathcal{R}})\cup\pi_{T}(\underline{B(T)})=P_{b}(f,{\mathcal{R}}).

As a consequence, the boundary points of
(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{C}}})
coincide with the boundary points of
(f,{\mathcal{R}}).

Item

ii)
. Let
p
be a boundary point of
{\mathcal{R}}_{S(\underline{B(T)})}
, and let
R\in{\mathcal{R}}_{S(\underline{B(T)})}
be a rectangle that contains
p
. Let
R_{0}\in{\mathcal{R}}
be a rectangle of
{\mathcal{R}}
that also contains
R
. After the
s
-boundary refinement:

(1)

If
p
belonged to both
\partial^{s}R_{0}
and
\partial^{u}R_{0}
, then
p
becomes a corner point of the rectangle
R
.

(2)

If
p
were in
\partial^{s}R_{0}
but not in
\partial^{u}R_{0}
, it remains in
\partial^{s}R
but not in
\partial^{u}R
. There is no change.

(3)

If
p
was in
\partial^{u}R_{0}
but not in
\partial^{s}R_{0}
, then it becomes a corner point of
R
.

Let
p
be a periodic boundary point in
{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{C}}}
. Consider a rectangle
R
in

{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{C}}}=[{\mathcal{R}}_{S(\underline{B(T)}}]_{U(\underline{B(T_{S(\underline{B(T)})})})},

that contains
p
, along with a rectangle
R_{0}
in
R_{0}\in{\mathcal{R}}_{S(\underline{B(T)})}
that contains
R
. After the
u
-boundary refinement, we can have two possible outcomes

(1)

If
p\in\partial^{s}R_{0}\cap\partial^{u}R_{0}
was a corner point of
R_{w_{0}}
, then the rectangle
R
has
p
as one of its corner points.

(2)

If
p\in\partial^{u}R_{0}\setminus\partial^{s}R_{0}
, after the
u
-boundary refinement,
p
is in
\partial^{s}R\cap\partial^{u}R
and is a corner point of
R
.

In conclusion, the geometric Markov partition
(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{C(T)})
exhibits the corner property, as asserted in Item
ii
.

Item

iii)
. This is a consequence of the algorithmic computation of the geometric types in the
s
and
u
boundary refinements, and the algorithmic computation of the family of boundary periodic codes for any geometric type, as explained in Subsection
7.1
.

∎

7.3.
The corner refinement along a family of periodic codes.

Let
{\mathcal{R}}
be a geometric Markov partition of
f:S\to S
with geometric type
T
that has the corner property. Take a family of periodic codes
{\mathcal{W}}\subset\Sigma_{A(T)}
and the family
P_{{\mathcal{W}}}=\pi_{T}({\mathcal{W}})
of periodic points for
f
. The
s
-boundary refinement of
{\mathcal{R}}
concerning this family yields
{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{W}})}
and its geometric type
T_{{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{W}})}
. The boundary points of
(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{W}}}
) are given by the union of the boundary points of
{\mathcal{R}}
and the periodic points determined by the projections of the codes in
{\mathcal{W}}
:

P_{b}(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})})=P_{b}(f,{\mathcal{R}})\cup P_{{\mathcal{W}}}

Let
{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathcal{W}}})}
be the corner refinement of
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
with geometric type
T_{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{W}})}
. A corollary of Proposition
15
, when applied to the geometric type
T_{S({\mathcal{W}})}
, is as follows,and in particular it implies Theorem
8
.

Corollary 20
.

Let
{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{W}})}
be the corner refinement of
(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})})
with geometric type
T_{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{W}})})
. Then:

i)

The boundary periodic points of
(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{W}}))})
and the boundary periodic points of
(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}})})
are exactly the same. So they are the union of the boundary periodic points of
(f,{\mathcal{R}})
such that are the protection of the family
{\mathcal{W}}
by
\pi_{(f,{\mathcal{R}})}
.

ii)

The Markov partition
(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{W}}))})
exhibits the corner property.

iii)

There are formulas and an algorithm to compute the geometric type
T_{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{W}})}
.

7.3.1.
The corner refinement in codes of bounded period

Finally, let us make a construction that will help us during the discussion of the Béguin algorithm (
[
Bég02
]
) in the article

Definition 79
.

Let
T
be a geometric type in the pseudo-Anosov class with a binary incidence matrix
A(T)
, and let
f:S\to S
be a generalized pseudo-Anosov homeomorphism with a geometric Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}
of geometric type
T
. Assume that
(f,{\mathcal{R}})
(and
T
) has the corner property. Now, consider the following definitions:

•

Let
P_{\underline{B(T)}}
be the maximum period among the boundary periodic codes of
T
:

P_{\underline{B(T)}}:=\max\{\textbf{Per}(\underline{w},\sigma_{T}):\underline{w}\in\underline{B(T)}\}

•

For any integer
P\geq P_{\underline{B(T)}}
, define
{\mathcal{W}}_{P}
as the set of admissible codes with periods less than or equal to
P
:

{\mathcal{W}}_{P}:=\{\underline{w}\in\Sigma_{A(T)}:\textbf{Per}(\underline{w},\sigma_{T})\leq P\}.

•

The corner refinement of
{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}}_{P})}
along the family
{\mathcal{W}}_{P}
is given by
{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{W}}_{P})}
and its geometric type is denoted as
T_{{\mathcal{C}}(P)}
.

Lemma 54
.

Let
P\geq P_{\underline{B(T)}}
. Suppose
p\in S
is a periodic point of
f
with a period less than or equal to
P
; i.e.,
\textbf{Per}(p,f)\leq P
. Consider
\underline{w}\in\Sigma_{A(T)}
as a code that projects to
p
:
\pi_{T}(\underline{w})=p
. Then, the period of
\underline{w}
is less than or equal to
P
, and
\underline{w}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{P}
.

Conversely, if
\underline{w}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{P}
, then the point
p:=\pi_{T}(\underline{w})\in S
is a periodic point of
f
with a period
\textbf{Per}(p,f)
less than or equal to
P
. In this manner:

(55)

\pi_{T}({\mathcal{W}}_{P})=\{p\in S:\textit{Per}(p,f)\leq P\}\text{ and }\pi_{T}^{-1}(\{p\in S:\textit{Per}(p,f)\leq P\})={\mathcal{W}}_{P}

Proof.

Let
p\in S
be a periodic point of
f
with period less or equal than
P
. We have two possible situations:

•

The periodic point
p
is a boundary point. In this case,
\underline{w}\in\underline{B(T)}\subset{\mathcal{W}}_{P}
just by definition of
{\mathcal{W}}_{P}
. Let’s recall that any code in
\underline{B(T)}
has a period less than or equal to
P_{\underline{B(T)}}\leq P
.

•

The periodic point
p
is interior. In this case,
\pi^{-1}(p)
contains a unique code
\underline{w}
whose period coincides with the period of
p
, i.e.,
\textbf{Per}(\underline{w},\sigma_{T})=\textbf{Per}(p,f)\leq P
. Therefore,
\underline{w}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{P}
.

This implies that:

(56)

\pi_{T}^{-1}(\{p\in S:\textit{Per}(p,f)\leq P\})\subset{\mathcal{W}}_{P}

and

(57)

\{p\in S:\textit{Per}(p,f)\leq P\}=\pi_{T}(\pi_{T}^{-1}(\{p\in S:\textit{Per}(p,f)\leq P\}))\subset\pi_{T}({\mathcal{W}}_{P}).

For any code
\underline{w}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{P}
with a period
Q
no greater than
P
, we can observe the following relationship:

p=\pi_{T}(\underline{w})=\pi_{T}(\sigma^{Q}(\underline{w}))=f^{Q}(\pi_{T}(\underline{w}))=f^{Q}(p)

This implies that
P\geq Q\geq\textbf{Per}(p,f)
, i.e., the period of
p
with respect to
f
is less than or equal to
P
. Then:

(58)

\pi_{T}({\mathcal{W}}_{P})\subset\{p\in S:\textit{Per}(p,f)\leq P\}.

This inclusion, along with the information provided in
57
, implies that:

\pi_{T}({\mathcal{W}}_{P})=\{p\in S:\textit{Per}(p,f)\leq P\}.

Furthermore, if
\underline{w}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{P}
, trivially
\underline{w}\in\pi_{T}^{-1}(\pi_{T}(\underline{w}))
and
p:=\pi_{T}(\underline{w})
has a period less than or equal to
P
. In this manner:

(59)

{\mathcal{W}}_{P}\subset\pi_{T}^{-1}(\{p\in S:\textit{Per}(p,f)\leq P\}).

Using this information and the contention provided in
56
, we establish the second equality of our lemma:

\pi_{T}^{-1}(\{p\in S:\textit{Per}(p,f)\leq P\})={\mathcal{W}}_{P}.

This ends our proof.
∎

Corollary 21
.

When
P\geq P_{B(T)}
, the periodic boundary points of the corner refinement
(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{C(T_{S({\mathcal{W}}_{P})})})
coincides with the set of all periodic points of
f
having periods less than or equal to
P
i.e.,

P_{b}(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{C(T_{S({\mathcal{W}}_{P})})})=\{p\in S:\textit{Per}(p,f)\leq P\}.

Furthermore,
(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{C(T_{S({\mathcal{W}}_{P})})})
also possesses the corner property.

Proof.

From Corollary
20
:

P_{b}(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{C(T_{S({\mathcal{W}}_{P})})})=P_{b}(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}}_{P})}).

But, we recall that:

P_{b}(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}}_{P})})=P_{b}(f,{\mathcal{R}})\cup\pi_{T}({\mathcal{W}}_{P}).

In view of Lemma
54

\pi_{T}({\mathcal{W}}_{P})=\{p\in S:\textit{Per}(p,f)\leq P\}

and clearly
P_{b}(f,{\mathcal{R}})\subset\{p\in S:\textit{Per}(p,f)\leq P\}
. Therefore:

P_{b}(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{C(T_{S({\mathcal{W}}_{P})})})=P_{b}(f,{\mathcal{R}}_{S({\mathcal{W}}_{P})})=P_{b}(f,{\mathcal{R}})\cup\pi_{T}({\mathcal{W}}_{P})=\{p\in S:\textit{Per}(p,f)\leq P\}.

∎
